Declaration of Originality
This thesis contains no material which has been accepted for a degree or diploma by the University or any
other institution, except by way of background information and duly acknowledged in the thesis, and to the
best of my knowledge and belief no material previously published or written by another person except where
due acknowledgement is made in the text of the thesis, nor does the thesis contain any material that infringes
copyright.
Statement of Authority of Access
The publishers of the papers comprising Chapters 2, 3 and 4 hold the copyright for that content. Access to the
material should be sought from the respective journals. The remaining non-published content of the thesis
not to be made available for loan or copying for two years following the date this statement was signed.
Following that time, the thesis may be made available for loan and limited copying and communication in
accordance with the Copyright Act 1968.

iii
Abstract
The Kerguelen Plateau is a biological hotspot and an important region for Southern Ocean ﬁsheries. However,
its remoteness and harsh environmental conditions make consistent sampling difﬁcult. Therefore, signiﬁcant
knowledge gaps remain regarding regional-scale impacts of ﬁshing and climate change. Very little is under-
stood about the transfer of energy from prey to predators on the Kerguelen Plateau and even less is understood
about how the food web is being impacted by ﬁshing pressure and will be impacted by future climate change.
Understanding the combined impacts of bottom-up and top-down processes on food web dynamics can help
inform ecosystem-based management on the Kerguelen Plateau. The research in this thesis compiles current
knowledge of the Kerguelen Plateau food web and uses Ecopath with Ecosim to explore ecosystem dynamics
and identify the potential impacts of climate change and ﬁshing over time and space.
Previously published work to develop the ﬁrst food web model with a balanced energy system for the Ker-
guelen Islands. This model forms the basis for the research in this thesis. Next, the entire plateau food web
was incorporated by extending the domain of the model to include not only the waters surrounding the Ker-
guelen Islands but also the region further south surrounding Heard and McDonald Islands. This model was
used to undertake a thorough exploration of food web structure and function, including answering questions
regarding the transfer of energy through the food web and interactions between the ﬁshery and the food web.
Results from this work indicate that killer whales, cephalopods (squid) and myctophids (lanternﬁsh) are key
pathways for the transfer of energy from lower trophic levels to top predators while ﬁshing activity (in the
form of catch and by-catch) had little impact on overall food web dynamics. This work provides the ﬁrst
quantitative description of the food web on the Kerguelen Plateau.
The Ecopath model was calibrated using time-series data, and the resulting Ecosim model was applied to
explore climate and ﬁshing impacts on the Kerguelen Plateau food web over the last 32 years (1986–2018).
Model results again showed that ﬁshing (ﬁshing mortality) was not a driver of food web dynamics. Instead,
analyses identiﬁed sea surface temperature, zonal wind speed and the Southern Annular Mode as drivers of
biomass trends in the food web. Additionally , results indicate that Patagonian toothﬁsh recruitment may be
sensitive to surface warming. These results highlight the importance of considering environmental change in
ecosystem-based management.
Spatial differences in environmental processes, ﬁshing pressure and marine protected areas (MPAs) on the
Kerguelen Plateau could have local-scale impacts on the food web. The Ecosim model was spatially resolved
by incorporating habitat maps in Ecospace. Food web dynamics were compared with ﬁshing and without
ﬁshing present to evaluate spatial ﬁshing impacts and food web distribution was evaluated in relation to the
current locations of MPAs. Observed spatial distributions were replicated in the model and ﬁndings showed
limited direct impacts of ﬁshing on Patagonian toothﬁsh and the food web. Modelled distributions showed
that groups in the food web were well distributed within MPAs, indicating that current ecosystem-based
management is representative of the food web.
This thesis presents the ﬁrst comprehensive analysis of food web dynamics on the Kerguelen Plateau. Results
from this thesis identiﬁed key energy pathways, potential environmental drivers and evaluated the degree of
protection provided by MPAs, all of which are important for conservation management and predicting South-
ern Ocean ecosystem response to change in the future. Findings suggest that current ﬁshing for Patagonian
toothﬁsh is operating at sustainable levels and spatial management is representative of the food web. This
work provides an important foundation for considering future ecosystem-based approaches, especially those
that include climate change.

"Nothing in this world that’s worth having comes easy."
- Dr Kelso, Scrubs, 2005.
I did just quote dialogue from a comedy series but I think it aptly describes my PhD experience, especially
over the last few months. I did have some incredible people around me that made it easier so I’ll keep this
short and sweet.
First, a massive thank you to my supervisors Stuart Corney , Jessica Melbourne-Thomas and Kerrie Swadling for
taking me on and providing an incredible amount of support, encouragement, guidance and most importantly ,
belief in my ability . I am deeply grateful for all of their help and advice. I would not have come this far
without them and there is no other group of supervisors I could have done this with. This PhD has allowed
me capitalise on so many opportunities that I don’t think I would have otherwise been given. There were so
many things I wanted to do when I started my PhD and with the help of my supervisors, I have been able to
do 99% of them.
A huge thank you to my family , having them close by has made this experience so much better. They have
never understood what I was doing (or why I was doing it), but they have always supported me. Thanks to
my sister and brother for keeping me laughing so I would keep my sanity . Thanks to my mum especially for
all the good food and for making my life a little bit easier for me during times of intense stress. Thanks to my
dad for keeping my car running, blessing my room after prayers and the many , many dad jokes. Thank you
to our dog Freddy , playing ball with you was the only time I truly never thought about my thesis.
Thank you to the people I have met during my PhD, it has been really helpful to have a group of supportive
people around that were going through the same things. Sometimes you get lucky and you meet amazing
people early on in your life. Thank you to Sheenal, for giving me a place to get away from it all. The biggest of
thank you’s to one of my dearest friends, Donna. Thanks for the dinner dates, movie nights and the incredible
amount of support and encouragement. Thank you for letting me vent your ear off about everything and never
letting me believe I couldn’t reach my goals.
Thank you to the scientists that have saved me a lot of googling and emailing. Thank you to Ben Raymond
and Mike Sumner for all of their help with R. Thank you to Philippe Ziegler for helping with data requests and
thank you to Cathy Bulman and Karen Alexander for their help with troubleshooting. Thank you to Donna
Harriss, her advice has always helped me feel better. Her techniques have not only helped me manage my
PhD but also improved my outlook on life and my future in marine science.
My grandma passed away quite suddenly during the write up of my thesis. She was an intelligent, strong,
independent woman. She undoubtedly passed those qualities on to my mum, who has (hopefully) passed
them on to me and she leaves behind a legacy of high-achieving women. I dedicate this work to her. Paati,
thank you for everything.

CHAPTER 1
Introduction
1.1 Southern Ocean food webs
The Southern Ocean covers∼10% of the world’s oceans and plays an important role in global climate reg-
ulation, ocean circulation and nutrient cycles (Turner et al., 2009; Xavier et al., 2016; Rogers et al., 2020).
Southern Ocean ecosystems have vital roles in these processes: primary productivity in the Southern Ocean
contributes signiﬁcantly to the uptake and export of CO2 (Rogers et al., 2020), microbes remineralise nutrients
from organic matter, and top predators have impacts on nutrient cycles via feeding and excretion (Ratnarajah
et al., 2014). In addition to their environmental services, Southern Ocean ecosystems support high-value
commercial ﬁsheries that target endemic species, including Antarctic krill, Antarctic toothﬁsh, Mackerel ice-
ﬁsh and Patagonian toothﬁsh (Mintenbeck, 2017). A detailed understanding of Southern Ocean food web
dynamics is needed to effectively conserve and manage Southern Ocean ecosystems. However, this under-
standing is difﬁcult to achieve due to the spatial heterogeneity of the processes driving food web dynamics in
the Southern Ocean.
1.1.1 Regional variation in Southern Ocean food webs
Southern Ocean food web structure and function is largely inﬂuenced by seasonality , sea ice cover and the
frontal systems of the Antarctic Circumpolar Current (ACC) (Constable et al., 2014; Hawkins et al., 2018;
Chapman et al., 2020). Close to the Antarctic continent, sea ice is an important driver of food web structure.
For example, in the Ross Sea where sea ice is dominant for much of the year, crystal krill (Euphausia crystal-
lorophias) and Antarctic silverﬁsh (Pleuragramma antarctica) are key prey species in the food web whereas
Antarctic krill are dominant in the Antarctic Peninsula where sea ice occurs seasonally (Constable and Doust,
2009; Murphy et al., 2013). Sea ice is also an important breeding habitat for some predators (e.g. crabeater
seals, Southwell et al. 2012). In open ocean areas, such as the sub-Antarctic (north of 60 ◦S), myctophids
(lanternﬁsh) and cephalopods are the dominant prey for predators and predators in these pelagic food webs
generally forage in regions adjacent to their breeding site (Thiers et al., 2017).
The ACC, driven eastward by strong westerly winds, transports high nutrient-low chlorophyll (HNLC) water
around Antarctica (Mintenbeck, 2017; Meredith et al., 2019). The ACC contains multiple water masses that
are separated by the fronts of the ACC. Each body of water has a distinct temperature, salinity and nutrient
concentration and is transported around the continent at separate latitudes (Orsi et al., 1995). Phytoplankton
blooms in the open ocean are observed in areas where the ACC interacts with bottom topography , resulting
in the upwelling of nutrient-rich water and iron enrichment (Sokolov and Rintoul, 2007). In the open ocean,
water temperature, mixed layer depth, light availability and nutrient availability inﬂuence the timing of phy-
toplankton blooms that support the food web (Constable et al., 2014). Differences in phytoplankton species,
zooplankton communities (Hunt and Hosie, 2005; Knox, 2007) and ﬁsh assemblages (De Broyer et al., 2014)
are associated with changing environmental properties at the latitudinal limits of the fronts (Constable et al.,
Chapter 1
2014), resulting in varied food web structures across the Southern Ocean.
The processes that impact organisms in the food web can generally be classiﬁed into two categories: processes
that affect productivity at the base of the food web and propagate upwards are classiﬁed as bottom-up while
those that directly affect higher trophic levels (i.e. predators) are considered top-down. Bottom-up processes
can cascade through the food web by reducing prey availability , leading to changes in breeding success,
foraging range or growth rates of predators (Meredith et al., 2019). Top-down processes, such as changes to
habitat or predation, can have ﬂow-on effects to the food web (Constable et al., 2014). For example, in the
western Ross Sea, a change in sea ice may have inﬂuenced the foraging behaviour of Adélie penguins, minke
whales and killer whales to increase predation pressure on their prey (crystal krill and Antarctic silverﬁsh).
Prey species populations were reduced as a result, lessening their grazing pressure on phytoplankton (Ainley
et al., 2006).
Fisheries in the Southern Ocean can act to remove key prey and predators from the food web, leading to
bottom-up and top-down impacts. For example, baleen whales are a key predator of Antarctic krill (Euphausia
superba). One hypothesis regarding the large decline of baleen whales via commercial whaling and increase of
other krill predators (seals, penguins and seabirds) suggests that this decline may have led to a "krill surplus".
This surplus may have allowed other krill predators to capitalise on the excess krill, increasing their fecundity
(known as the "krill surplus hypothesis", Laws 1977). Findings from a food web modelling study suggest
that while the "krill surplus hypothesis" is a plausible explanation of observed trends, it is also likely that the
excess krill was depleted in response to a 20-year period of low primary productivity . This is in line with an
alternative hypothesis suggesting that the decline in baleen whales led to a decrease in krill due to less iron
recycling through their faeces (Nicol et al., 2010). Current commercial ﬁshing of Antarctic krill could have
impacts on predator performance (e.g. foraging-trip duration and breeding success) if local harvest rates are
high (Watters et al., 2020).
In addition to varied food web structures across the Southern Ocean, regional-scale effects from climate
change have been observed and are expected to continue. Surface waters in the northern part of the Southern
Ocean have warmed since the 1980s while surface waters in the southern part have cooled and freshened
(Meredith et al., 2019). Circumpolar winds have strengthened over recent decades (since the 1960s–1970s),
and eddy ﬁelds have intensiﬁed (since the early 1990s) (Meredith et al., 2019). Analyses of climate trends
show that the west side of Antarctica (most noticeably the Antarctic Peninsula) has experienced warming
and a signiﬁcant decrease in sea ice concentration while the east side has shown both regions of increase
and regions of no change (Parkinson, 2019). Climate change impacts on pelagic primary production remain
uncertain due to limited data availability (Meredith et al., 2019) therefore, knowledge of potential bottom-
up impacts is limited. Model projections indicate that decreased summer mixed layer depth and increased
iron supply may promote phytoplankton growth between 40–50◦S while deepening of summer mixed layer
depth and decreased light availability during summer may decrease primary productivity between 50–65◦S
(Meredith et al., 2019). If these trends continue in the future, they will likely inﬂuence the spatial distribution
of predators and prey . Long-term climate indices such as the El Niño–Southern Oscillation (operating on
subdecadal timescales) or the Southern Annular Mode (more variable in time - weekly , or decadal periods) are
also important for understanding habitat variability and changes to food web dynamics over time (Constable
et al., 2014).
Ecological responses to climate change are well-documented for the West Antarctic Peninsula (Paciﬁc sector
south of 60◦, described below) and South Atlantic (Atlantic sector, described below). Responses to present
2
Chapter 1
rates of change include changes to primary productivity associated with shallow mixed layer depth and al-
tered duration of the sea ice season (Meredith et al., 2019). Future warming may result in habitat loss for a
dominant myctophid species (Electrona antarctica) and a poleward shift in habitat for ﬁsh species that have
narrow thermal tolerances (Meredith et al. 2019 and references therein). Predator impacts are regionally
variable and primarily related to sea ice and prey availability (Meredith et al., 2019). For example, a south-
ward shift in foraging habitat has increased travel distance and foraging depth for king penguins, resulting in
a population decrease (Bost et al., 2015). Seabird populations have exhibited both negative and positive im-
pacts from surface warming (Table 1.1) and a change in wind pattern has inﬂuenced albatross travel rate and
ﬂight speed (Weimerskirch et al., 2012). Additionally , changes to the oceanographic and topographic prop-
erties that control prey aggregation and distribution are likely to inﬂuence the responses of marine mammals
to climate change (Meredith et al., 2019).
The Indian sector (non-shaded region in Fig 1.1a) is under-sampled in comparison to the Antarctic Peninsula,
creating a gap in our understanding of Southern Ocean ecosystem response to spatio-temporal drivers of
change. A large proportion of the Indian sector is HNLC. The Kerguelen Plateau, in contrast, is a biological
hotspot in this region that has remained understudied due largely to its remote location.
1.2 The Kerguelen Plateau
The Kerguelen Plateau, extending from 46◦S–64◦S along the 75◦E meridian (Bestley et al., 2020), is a large
oceanic plateau in the Indian sector of the Southern Ocean (Fig 1.1a). The northern section (north of 56◦S), is
the focus of this study (Fig 1.1b). The Kerguelen Plateau forms a large topographical barrier to the eastward
ﬂow of the ACC (Park et al., 2014). The ACC is diverted as it encounters the shallow bathymetry of the plateau
and is enriched with iron from the shelf sediments (Della Penna et al., 2018). Vertical mixing associated
with internal tides brings iron into the euphotic zone, stimulating phytoplankton growth (Park et al., 2008).
Blooms occur annually during spring and summer on the east side of the Kerguelen Plateau and downstream
when mixed layer depth is shallow as a result of less intense winds and warmer surface water (Koubbi et al.,
2016). These blooms contain up to 10 times more chlorophyll than the surrounding waters (Della Penna
et al., 2018), which concentrates prey (zooplankton, ﬁsh, cephalopods) and provides prime foraging areas
for breeding and non-breeding higher predators (Koubbi et al., 2016).
1.2.1 Kerguelen Plateau food web
The remoteness of the islands on the Kerguelen Plateau from other land masses means that these islands are
crucial breeding sites for many seabirds and seals east of 60◦E in the Indian sector (Hindell et al., 2011). The
area has a high occurrence of endemic species (Rogers et al., 2020) and habitat diversity (kelp forests, mussel
beds, reefs and fjords, Féral et al. 2019). Also occurring on the plateau are benthic habitats that support slow
growing and vulnerable species (Féral et al., 2019) and essential nurseries and spawning habitat for ﬁshes
(including those targeted by ﬁsheries, Koubbi et al. 2016; Féral et al. 2019; Duhamel et al. 2019; Ziegler and
Welsford 2019).
General food web structure can be inferred from species distribution studies, diet studies and ecoregionalisa-
tion studies (Murphy et al., 2016; Koubbi et al., 2016). Energy from phytoplankton is transferred to copepods,
the dominant mesozooplankton group (Carlotti et al., 2015), which are consumed by macrozooplankton con-
sisting mainly of euphausiids, amphipods, and chaetognaths (Hunt et al., 2011). Major predators in the
3
Chapter 1
Figure 1.1. Map of the Southern Ocean and study domain. a) Map illustrating Antarctica and the Southern
Ocean. The Kerguelen Plateau is shown by the white box located in the Indian sector (non-shaded region), b)
Map illustrating the Kerguelen Plateau and Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZs). Hatched area shows the French
EEZ and striped area shows the Australian EEZ. Blue icons correspond to the targeted species described in
Fig 1.2 and orange icons correspond to the ﬁshing gears in operation. Trawling is represented by the ﬁshing
net and longline ﬁshing is represented by the hook. Frontal positions as described in Park et al. (2014): SAF
– subantarctic front, PF – polar front, SACCF – southern ACC front.
region are the species of seals and seabirds that breed on the islands (listed in Chapter 3), while whales and
dolphins also visit the area to feed. Zooplankton, ﬁsh and cephalopods are frequently reported in predator
diets indicating that these groups are important components of the food web (Cherel et al., 2005).
While a general understanding of the food web has been established, the relative importance of major energy
pathways has not yet been identiﬁed and attempts to quantify and understand food web dynamics in detail
have remained incomplete. An initial attempt at a food web model was previously developed to investigate
food web dynamics on the Kerguelen Islands however, as the model was not ﬁnished, the study has been
incomplete for more than a decade (Pruvost et al., 2005). Moreover, bottom-up and top-down drivers of
ecosystem dynamics remain unexplored despite evidence showing climate and ﬁshing impacts on species in
the food web (Table 1.1).
1.2.2 Commercial ﬁshing on the Kerguelen Plateau
The Kerguelen Plateau is comprised of two territories, both of which have a history of ﬁshing. The Kerguelen
Islands in the north are part of the French territory and Heard and McDonald Islands are part of the Australian
territory (Fig 1.1b).
Trawl ﬁshing in the French territory began in the 1970s targeting mackerel iceﬁsh, marbled rockcod and
grey rockcod without any management (Pruvost et al., 2005). In 1978, the French Exclusive Economic Zone
(EEZ; hatched region in Fig 1.1b) was established to manage the ﬁshery . Patagonian toothﬁsh became a tar-
4
Chapter 1
get species in 1985, following the discovery of a deep-sea stock (Duhamel et al., 2019; Mintenbeck, 2017).
Trawl ﬁshing for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Fig 1.2) continued until longline ﬁshing was introduced in 1991 and
gradually replaced trawling. The ﬁshery has been solely longline since 2001. The high economic value of
Patagonian toothﬁsh attracted illegal, unregulated and unreported (IUU) ﬁshing from 1997–2004 (Minten-
beck, 2017; Duhamel et al., 2019), but since this time the ﬁshery has been well-managed.
In 1979, Australia implemented an Australian Fishing Zone, which became an EEZ in 1994 (Ziegler and
Welsford, 2019) (striped area in Fig 1.1b). Commercial ﬁshing for Patagonian toothﬁsh began in 1997 as a
trawl ﬁshery . Longline ﬁshing was implemented in 2003 and gradually replaced trawling (Subramaniam et al.,
2020c) over the next few years. Signiﬁcant levels of IUU ﬁshing also occurred within the EEZ between the late
1990s and early 2000s (Ziegler and Welsford, 2019), but this has now been largely controlled. Commercial
trawling for mackerel iceﬁsh (Fig 1.2) began in 1997 and continues to this day (Maschette and Welsford,
2019).
The Commission for the Conservation of Antarctic Marine Living Resources (CCAMLR), signed in 1980 and
enforced in 1982 (Mintenbeck, 2017; Constable et al., 2000), applies an ecosystem-based approach to manag-
ing Southern Ocean ﬁsheries. Conservation principles are used to manage ﬁsheries such that negative ﬁshing
impacts are reversible over a ﬁxed period. These principles are designed to ensure that the population size
and recruitment stability of target species and ecological relationships between target and non-target species
are maintained, and to prevent and minimise the risk of irreversible changes to marine ecosystems (Constable
et al., 2000). Both EEZs on the Kerguelen Plateau occur within the area covered by the CCAMLR treaty and
while Australian catch limits are based on management advice from CCAMLR (Ziegler and Welsford, 2019),
French catch limits are decided unilaterally . Both target species (Patagonian toothﬁsh and mackerel iceﬁsh)
appear to have important roles in the transfer of energy through the food web and are likely to be sensitive to
a changing climate and prey availability (Fig 1.2, Maschette and Welsford 2019; Belchier and Collins 2008).
Developing an understanding of their roles in the food web is important for ecosystem-based management.
Negative impacts have been observed on non-target species that have interacted with the ﬁshery . Incidental
seabird mortality from ﬁshing has occurred within both EEZs as a consequence of commercial and IUU ﬁshing.
Due to their long life cycles, this can have lasting impacts on populations. Strategies to mitigate this impact,
including scaring devices, night-setting, seasonal and areal closures, weighted lines and policing IUU ﬁshing,
have been very effective (Duhamel et al., 2019). Fish by-catch limits are set in both EEZs to reduce negative
impacts on by-catch populations (CCAMLR, 2018a,b). Recent evidence of sperm whales and killer whales
removing toothﬁsh from longlines (known as depredation) may impact these populations in the future with
ﬂow-on effects for the ﬁshery and the food web (Tixier et al., 2020).
Marine protected areas (MPAs), including no-take regions, have been implemented relatively recently (early
to mid 2000s) to protect vulnerable benthic ecosystems and foraging areas for predators (Azam et al., 2019;
Brooks et al., 2019). MPAs can be a very effective spatial tool for conserving, protecting and managing
Southern Ocean ecosystems, however studies suggest that sufﬁcient food web representation within the MPA
is needed for successful management, especially in no-take areas (Brooks et al., 2020; Sala and Giakoumi,
2017).

1.2.3 Combined ﬁshing and climate change impacts
Collectively , ﬁshing for Patagonian toothﬁsh and mackerel iceﬁsh has occurred over a 35-year period. Climate
change impacts have also been observed over this time and there is evidence to suggest that the combined
effects of ﬁshing and climate change have had, and will continue to have, impacts on organisms on the
Kerguelen Plateau. However, current understanding regarding the consequences of climate change for ﬁnﬁsh
ﬁsheries such as those on the plateau are limited and likely to be related to food web dynamics (Meredith
et al., 2019).
Table 1.1 summarises observed and projected climate and ﬁshing impacts on species on the Kerguelen Plateau.
These studies demonstrate the importance of considering the combined impacts of climate and ﬁshing on the
food web for successful ecosystem-based management. As most impacts have been linked to prey availability ,
these studies further emphasise the importance of understanding the relative importance of energy pathways
in the food web for resource management.

Chapter 1
1.2.4 Food web modelling
The heterogeneity in Southern Ocean processes that inﬂuence regional food web dynamics is complex and
understanding is often hindered by limited data availability . Some parts of the Southern Ocean are sampled
more frequently than others, enabling ﬁrmer conclusions to be reached regarding ecosystem change over time.
In other regions of the Southern Ocean, where sampling is less frequent, advancing our overall understanding
of regional-scale impacts requires tools that can not only capture food web complexity with patchy data over
space and time, but can also be used to answer questions about ecosystem change. Food web models are one
of these tools (Collie et al., 2016).
Food web models vary in the parameters required, the number of parameters used and the equations or
relationships used to describe the food web (Plagányi, 2007). They can also include environmental parameters
or ﬁshing activity as drivers of ecosystem change. This thesis used Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) to understand
the spatio-temporal drivers of change for the Kerguelen Plateau ecosystem. EwE is a food web modelling
tool that has been used to explore many regional representations of Southern Ocean food webs although
most of this effort has been focused on Atlantic and Paciﬁc food webs (Fig 1.3). A detailed description of
EwE is provided in all chapters, therefore here we focus on the beneﬁts of using EwE for evaluating potential
spatio-temporal drivers of ecosystem change on the Kerguelen Plateau.
Other whole of ecosystem models, such as Atlantis, are data heavy (Pethybridge et al., 2019). EwE can be
applied to data poor regions as it estimates values where there are gaps in the data based on interactions
in the food web. EwE models can also be ﬁtted to time-series data to explore combined environmental and
ﬁshing impacts, and the model package includes a spatial component to explore spatially resolved impacts
and evaluate the effectiveness of MPAs. Additionally , this model can be used for comparison with existing
EwE models developed for the Southern Ocean to quantitatively compare regional food web dynamics.

Chapter 1
1.3 Thesis structure
This study aims to improve the current understanding of food web dynamics on the Kerguelen Plateau and
investigate the combined spatio-temporal effects of ﬁshing and environmental change on the food web. This
is achieved by developing the ﬁrst time-dynamic, spatially explicit EwE model for the Kerguelen Plateau.
This thesis aims to address the following knowledge gaps (Table 1.2):
Table 1.2. Knowledge gaps addressed in this thesis
Knowledge gaps
Major energy pathways and key groups for energy transfer in the
food web
Fishing as a top-down driver of ecosystem change: the role of
target ﬁsh species in the food web and the temporal and spatial
impacts of ﬁshing
Predation as a top-down driver of food web dynamics: predators
with strong top-down inﬂuence on the food web at spatial and
temporal scales
Understand environmental drivers of food web dynamics over
time
Ecosystem-based management: impacts of ﬁshing on non-target
species (e.g. by-catch) and effectiveness of MPAs
This thesis is structured as follows:
Chapter 2describes the completion of a previously unbalanced Ecopath model for the French EEZ surrounding
the Kerguelen Islands (described in section 1.2.1). This model was published in 2005 and has been unbalanced
(i.e. non-calibrated, as mass-balance was not achieved) for more than a decade. We provide preliminary
insights into food web dynamics using Ecopath tools and provide all relevant data for recreating the model.
Chapter 3describes the extension of the model domain in Chapter 2 to include the Australian EEZ surrounding
Heard and McDonald Islands. This model provides a time-averaged exploration of Kerguelen Plateau food
web dynamics using Ecopath. Published data on food web structure and function were gathered from the
literature providing the ﬁrst compilation of available data for the region and the ﬁrst insights into energy
pathways and key functional groups. Modelled groups from Chapter 2 are restructured and data are updated
to provide a detailed analysis of energy transfer in the food web. Finally , ﬁsheries data are added to explore
the impact of commercial ﬁshing on target and non-target species.
Chapter 4 extends the model from Chapter 3 using Ecosim to explore temporal food web drivers. The model
is ﬁtted to time-series data of biomass and ﬁshing activity using a step-wise ﬁtting approach to investigate
10
Chapter 1
climate and ﬁshing impacts on the food web over a 32-year period (1986–2018). Model output was correlated
with environmental parameters to identify potential drivers of ecosystem change.
Chapter 5 resolves the time-dynamic model from Chapter 4 on a grid and tuned using habitat modelling
and species tracking studies. Food web dynamics were compared between non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed scenarios
to explore the spatial impacts of ﬁshing and biomass distribution was evaluated to consider the degree of
ecosystem protection provided by MPAs.
Chapter 6 summarises the ﬁndings in this thesis with respect to knowledge gaps and the implications of these
ﬁndings. This chapter also outlines possible future work with the models developed in this thesis and how
similar work can inform management strategies for Southern Ocean ecosystems.
A summary of the modelling approach used in this study to address knowledge gaps is presented in Fig 1.4.
Figure 1.4. Model development approach using EwE to address current knowledge gaps. Models were
developed in steps corresponding with Chapters 2–5. First, a model was developed to capture food web
dynamics, second, the model was further developed to incorporate variability over time, lastly , the model was
spatially resolved to capture variability over space.
11
CHAPTER 2
A mass-balanced model for the Kerguelen Islands
Abstract
Ecosystem models are useful tools for making informed management decisions regarding the potential effects
of environmental change and ﬁshing activity on Southern Ocean ecosystems. Recent work has successfully
updated an existing Ecopath model for the Kerguelen Exclusive Economic Zone from an unbalanced to a
balanced model state (where energy inputs equal outputs). This balanced model details food web dynamics
in the Kerguelen Island region using species biomass and diet information. In this paper we present this
balanced model, as well as an evaluation of food web structure and function using tools found in Ecopath.
Energy ﬂows in the model suggest that the Kerguelen Island food web is smaller and more mature than other
sub-Antarctic food webs and the impacts of direct and indirect interactions on functional groups highlighted
the importance of cephalopods in the food web. This new model has been expanded to include the entire
northern region of the Kerguelen Plateau.
2.1 Introduction
The ability to make predictions about the effects of environmental change, shifts in productivity and ﬁshing
on marine ecosystems requires an understanding of food web dynamics. This in turn relies on ecosystem
models as a tool to evaluate food web structure and function. The ecosystem on the Kerguelen Plateau
supports valuable French and Australian ﬁsheries that target Patagonian toothﬁsh (Dissostichus eleginoides)
and Mackerel iceﬁsh (Champsocephalus gunnari). The ﬁshery interacts with the food web through the removal
of target species, by-catch, and by providing an easy food source for apex predators (Guinet et al., 2015). The
Kerguelen Plateau is a breeding ground for ﬂying seabirds, penguins, and seals, all of which consume large
quantities of ﬁsh and squid as part of their diet. Despite the high occurrence of biological activity on the
plateau, food web dynamics are still poorly understood.
Ecopath with Ecosim is a widely used software platform for developing ecosystem models (Christensen and
Walters, 2004). This study is conﬁned to the Ecopath module of Ecopath with Ecosim. Ecopath provides
a static view of the food web averaged over a known time period. Ecopath uses four parameters to de-
scribe species in the ecosystem: biomass (B), consumption /biomass (Q/B), production/biomass (P/B), and
ecotrophic efﬁciency (EE). The model is balanced by adjusting parameters (biomass, consumption/biomass,
production/biomass) until the EE of each group (the proportion of production that is used within the system)
is less than one. Note that detritus groups do not require any additional parameters except for biomass. The
EEs for detritus groups are deﬁned as the ratio between ﬂows into and out of the group. Under steady-state
assumption, this ratio should be equal to one (Christensen and Walters, 2004). Full details on balancing
Ecopath models can be found in Christensen and Walters (2004).
Pruvost et al. (2005) presented a preliminary Ecopath model to describe food web dynamics in the Kerguelen
Chapter 2
Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ), however the energy inputs and outputs of the model were unbalanced.
Balancing the energy in an Ecopath (or Ecopath with Ecosim) model is crucial to obtaining interpretable
estimates of energy ﬂow in the food web. This study presents a balanced version of that model and a basic
evaluation of model parameters. We aimed to provide a balanced version of the model that is as close as
possible to the original model and therefore have not included ﬁsheries data. Our balanced model represents
the mean state of the ecosystem in the absence of ﬁshing. This model domain is currently being extended to
include the entire northern region of the Kerguelen Plateau.
2.2 Methods
Ecopath model parameters were taken from Table 19 in Pruvost et al. (2005) and modiﬁed prior to balancing.
Patagonian toothﬁsh were split into two separate functional groups based on the segregation seen in the
water column. We have maintained this separation in our model, one group represented juvenile toothﬁsh
and the other represented adult toothﬁsh. Following Pruvost et al. (2005), we have also opted not to use
the multistanza option for entering juvenile and adult groups of the same species to reproduce the model as
closely as possible to the original.
Names of some functional groups were changed to better describe the species in these groups. “Filtering
mammals” is now “Baleen whales”, “Hunting mammals” is now “Seals and dolphins”, “Surface seabirds” is
now “Flying seabirds”, “Diving seabirds” is now “Penguins”. Note that the biomass value for ﬁltering mammals
presented in Table 19 of Pruvost et al. (2005) is incorrect (Pruvost, pers comm.). In this model we use the
correct biomass value from Table 16b in Pruvost et al. (2005) for ﬁltering mammals. Two functional groups
were added to the Pruvost et al. (2005) model prior to balancing: a bacteria group, and a carcass group to
represent mortality from higher trophic predators that is not consumed. If more than one detritus group is
speciﬁed in Ecopath, then the detritus fate form must be completed.
Following the Ecopath model for the Ross Sea (Pinkerton et al., 2010), unassimilated consumption was di-
rected to detritus fate, and other mortality was directed to the carcass group. Fractions used to describe
other mortality for functional groups were taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010). These values are presented in
Table 2.1 and changes to the data are highlighted in bold. Parameters were adjusted in Ecopath to achieve
mass-balance.
This balanced model was tested using previously published guidelines for assessing Ecopath parameters (Link,
2010). These guidelines, referred to as pre-balance diagnostics, provide methods for assessing Ecopath pa-
rameters prior to balancing models. One method from this guide was used to assess biomass, speciﬁcally the
span and magnitude of increasing biomass with decreasing trophic level. Link (2010) suggests that biomass
(on a log-scale), should span 5-7 orders of magnitude and increase 5-10% with decreasing trophic level.
Once parameters were evaluated, a preliminary look at the food web dynamics in the model was conducted
using ecosystem descriptors provided in Ecopath with Ecosim.

Pre-balance diagnostics for balanced biomass estimates show an increase in the order of 15% with decreasing
trophic level (blue line in Figure 2.1). Groups far above or under this line included sharks, small pelagic ﬁshes
and deep-benthic omnivores (orange points in Figure 2.1). Biomass estimates were taken from sources not
speciﬁc to the Kerguelen Island region as there are very few data for these groups on the Kerguelen Plateau.
As balanced biomass estimates generally followed the guideline for trophic decomposition, we consider these
estimates appropriate for use in the Ecopath model.
Summary statistics describe ecosystem size and maturity in terms of ﬂows and can be compared with summary
15
Chapter 2
Figure 2.1. Pre-balance diagnostics for balanced biomass estimates. Blue – line of best ﬁt, red – pre-balance
guideline. Groups that depart far from the slope are shown as orange dots.
statistics from other Ecopath models (Table 2.3). Summary statistics were compared to Ecopath models for the
Prince Edwards Islands, South Georgia, South Shetland Islands, Falkland Islands and the Antarctic Peninsula
(Bredesen, 2003; Cheung and Pitcher, 2005; Erfan and Pitcher, 2005; Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana, 2008;
Hill et al., 2012; Hoover, 2012; Gurney et al., 2014) (Figure 2.2). The size of the system was compared to
other sub-Antarctic models using total system throughput and total biomass. Total system throughput in the
model was 5331 tonnes km−2 y-1, and total biomass (excluding detritus) was 92 tonnes km−2. Compared to
the range in values reported for other sub-Antarctic models, the size of this system is smaller.
System maturity and complexity was assessed using total primary production /total respiration, net system
production, total primary production/total biomass, and total biomass/total throughput. Values close to one
for total primary production /total respiration and zero for net system production indicate a mature system
where energy is balanced between production and respiration. Total primary production /total respiration
in the balanced model was 1.03, showing energy in this system is close to being balanced. Net system pro-
duction is the difference between total primary production and total respiration. In contrast to total primary
production/total respiration, our value of 31 tonnes km −2 y-1 for net system production suggests that the
system is still developing. However, compared to other sub-Antarctic food webs our value is much lower
(Figure 2.2). Low values for total primary production /total biomass indicate a mature system. Compared to
16
Chapter 2
other models in Figure 2.2, our value of 12.28 suggests that our system is more mature. Low values for total
biomass/total throughput indicate a developing system. Our value of 0.017 indicates the system is still de-
veloping. However, in comparison to other models our system is more mature (Figure 2). Overall, summary
statistics suggest that the food web on the plateau is smaller and more mature than other sub-Antarctic food
webs.

was included (4.2%). Transfer efﬁciencies from primary producers were most efﬁcient in trophic levels II and

model, an increase in the biomass of top predators negatively affected seals and dolphins, ﬂying seabirds,
penguins and sharks (Figure 2.3). This is expected, as top predators are one of the few predators preying
on these groups (see diet matrix, Table 2.5). An increase in the biomass of top predators has positive effects
on large pelagic ﬁshes (Figure 2.3). This is in line with expectations as the predators of this group are the
seabirds. An increase in the biomass of top predators would increase their consumption of seabirds, and
reduce seabirds consuming large pelagic ﬁshes. An increase in the biomass of cephalopod groups negatively
affects seals and dolphins, ﬂying seabirds, penguins and sharks. This result is unexpected and could be a
result of competition with ﬁsh groups for food as they consume the same prey . Fish are a major component in
the diet of these groups and increasing cephalopod biomass could introduce competition for food, reducing
prey availability for predator groups (Table 2.5).
Figure 2.3. Mixed trophic impact plot. Diagram shows the positive (blue) or negative (red) impact of an
increase in the biomass of the impacting functional group on the impacted functional group.

2.4 Discussion and conclusion
The increased biomass value of 7.4 t km −2 for phytoplankton is comparable to Ecopath models for sub-
Antarctic islands in the Indian Ocean and in situ measurements on the plateau. A biomass of 8.51 t km −2
was used for phytoplankton in the Ecopath model for the Prince Edward Islands (Gurney et al., 2014). Aver-
age chlorophyll a (chl a) biomass for the south-west side of the Kerguelen Plateau from early spring to late
summer (stations A13-A18) was 0.184 mg chl a m −3 Becquevort et al. (2000). Assuming a chl a to carbon
conversion of 35 (Dehairs et al., 1992; Fiala et al., 1998b) and that carbon is 10% of wet weight (Dalsgaard
et al., 1997), this converts to a biomass of 7.7 t km−2. Sampling at the Kerﬁx station on the south-west side
of the plateau showed a range of 15–85 mg chla m−2, which converts to a biomass range of 5.3–29.8 t km−2
(Fiala et al., 1998). A long-term analysis of satellite chl a data for the entire Kerguelen Plateau showed a
range of 0.2–0.7 mg chla m−3, converting to a biomass range of 8.4–29.4 t km−2 (Dragon et al., 2011). This
suggests that the balanced value is on the lower end of the range of phytoplankton biomass estimates for the
area.
The pre-balance guideline is a relatively new method that has been used in recent Ecopath models (Heymans
et al. 2016 and references therein). In the balanced model, top predator biomass is lower than adult toothﬁsh
biomass, which may undermine the interpretations of model outcomes for ﬁsheries management. The top
predators group consists of orcas and sperm whales and is not the only group preying on toothﬁsh in the
model. The collective biomass of these groups (top predators, seals and dolphins, ﬂying seabirds, and sharks)
is still slightly lower than toothﬁsh biomass however, this is also seen in the Ecopath model for the Ross
Sea (Pinkerton et al., 2010). In other models where toothﬁsh is also a separate functional group, predator
biomasses are only slightly higher than toothﬁsh biomass (Hill et al., 2012; Gurney et al., 2014). Additionally ,
as these predators are transient, their biomass is weighted by the time spent away from the model domain,
resulting in a lower biomass in the model. We are therefore conﬁdent that these biomass values are reasonable.
Estimating biomass for sharks, small pelagic ﬁsh, and benthic groups is generally difﬁcult. However, upon
checking the groups identiﬁed in the biomass plot, estimates for these groups were reasonable and the best
available estimate at the time. There have been no targeted studies to estimate shark biomass, as they are not
common in the Southern Ocean (Cherel and Duhamel, 2004). Shark biomass in this model was assumed to
be 1% of ﬁsh biomass, giving a value of 0.001 t km−2 (Pruvost et al., 2005). The biomass estimate for small
pelagic ﬁsh is comparable to biomass estimates of similar ﬁsh functional groups in other Ecopath models. Hill
et al. (2012) use a value of 9.2 t km−2 for pelagic ﬁsh in their balanced model and Gurney et al. (2014) use
the same value as Pruvost et al. (2005) for their pelagic ﬁsh groups indicating that the value for the balanced
model is sufﬁcient. Benthic biodiversity has been recently investigated in the Kerguelen region but biomass
has not been surveyed since the 1970s (Améziane et al., 2011; Féral and Poulin, 2011). One study found
benthic species composition and biomass off Marion Island were similar to the Kerguelen Islands, reporting
that total zoobenthic biomass ranged from 68 to 598 g m-−2 at Marion Island and 61 g m−2 at Kerguelen Island
(Beckley and Branch, 1992). This value is high but still compares to total zoobenthic biomass in the balanced
model (collective biomass of benthic groups 15-17 = 41.8 t km−2), indicating this value is reasonable.
The mixed trophic impact plot in this study illustrated its usefulness in understanding food web dynamics in
the system. Most impacts were expected and easily explained by the diet matrix however one unexpected
result from the mixed trophic impact plot was the negative impact an increase in the biomass of cephalopods
had on the biomass of seals and dolphins, ﬂying seabirds, penguins, and sharks. There is a lot of overlap in the
21
Chapter 2
diet between cephalopod groups and these predator groups, small pelagic ﬁsh collectively make up to 25%
of cephalopod diets, and up to 36% of predator diets, with 18.5% belonging to ﬂying seabirds. Zooplankton
groups make up to 45% of cephalopod diets, 78% of penguins diet and 21% of ﬂying seabirds diet. An increase
in the biomass of cephalopods could introduce competition for resources in the model, negatively impacting
predators. Especially penguins, as 95% of their diet overlaps with cephalopod diets (Table 2). One of the
limitations of mixed trophic impacts is that it does not account for prey-switching in response to changes in
biomass. These interactions could be further explored in Ecosim, a time dynamic module in Ecopath with
Ecosim that has the capacity to accommodate prey-switching.
This work presents the ﬁrst mass-balanced ecosystem model for the Kerguelen EEZ. In the next implementa-
tion of this model (Chapter 3), the model domain has been extended to include Heard and McDonald Islands.
Functional groups were re-organised to provide more resolution, the multi-stanza grouping was used to de-
ﬁne Patagonian toothﬁsh, and speciﬁc groups were deﬁned for species targeted by the ﬁshery . Fisheries catch
was added to this extended version of the model, biomass and diet data were updated wherever possible, and
data speciﬁc to the Kerguelen Plateau were used where available. The plateau-wide version of the model was
used to evaluate the combined effects of ﬁshing and climate change on the food web.
22
CHAPTER 3
Exploring ecosystem structure and function of
the northern Kerguelen Plateau using a
mass-balanced food web model
Abstract
Annual phytoplankton blooms on the northern region of the Kerguelen Plateau fuel a productive food web that
supports highly valuable commercial ﬁsheries for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Dissostichus eleginoides) and mackerel
iceﬁsh (Champsocephalus gunnari). The food web on the plateau is understudied in comparison to other re-
gions of the Southern Ocean. Major linkages and energy pathways have not been explored, and the combined
effects of ﬁshing and a changing climate on the ecosystem are largely unknown. Single species studies on
the plateau have shown that the combined effects of climate change and ﬁsheries are impacting populations
however, it is unclear how these impacts translate to the ecosystem. We extended an existing Ecopath model
to describe food web dynamics on the plateau and investigate food web interactions with the ﬁshery . Results
from our model highlight, for the ﬁrst time, the properties of the food web, major energy pathways and en-
ergy transfers between trophic levels. Energy transfer from detritus was most efﬁcient at the lowest trophic
level while energy from primary production was more efﬁcient at higher trophic levels. Consumption and
respiration were high in our system, most likely due to the inclusion of bacteria and microzooplankton. Killer
whales, cephalopods and myctophids were key functional groups for energy transfer in the system. These
groups were relatively data poor, suggesting a useful focus area for future updates to the model. Patagonian
toothﬁsh and mackerel iceﬁsh were heavily consumed in the food web, however, the inclusion of ﬁsheries
catches and by-catch had little to no impact on food web dynamics.
3.1 Introduction
The Kerguelen Plateau is a large oceanic plateau in the Southern Ocean (Fig 3.1). Extending 2200 km in a
northwest to southeast direction, the plateau is the largest impediment to the eastward ﬂow of the Antarctic
Circumpolar Current. As a consequence, the plateau intersects with several different water masses circulating
Antarctica (Park et al., 2014). The northern region of the plateau encompasses the Kerguelen Islands and
Heard and McDonald Islands, and is separated from the southern region by the Fawn Trough (Park et al.,
2014). High nutrient-low chlorophyll (HNLC) water passes to the north of the Kerguelen Islands and ﬂows
south-east where it is fertilised with iron from the shelves, resulting in annual phytoplankton blooms that fuel
the food web (Park et al., 2014).
The northern region of the Kerguelen Plateau, hereafter referred to as the Kerguelen Plateau (or plateau),
covers two territories: the French territory around Kerguelen Island and the Australian territory around
Chapter 3
Heard and McDonald Islands. France and Australia operate separate ﬁsheries that target Patagonian toothﬁsh
(Dissostichus eleginoides) in their respective Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZ), and mackerel iceﬁsh (Champ-
socephalus gunnari) in the Heard and McDonald EEZ (Fig 3.1). The ﬁshery is managed by the Commission
for the Conservation of Antarctic Marine Living Resources (CCAMLR), which employs an ecosystem-based
approach to managing the ﬁsheries (Constable et al., 2000). Catch limits and ﬁshing procedures are adjusted
by considering the negative impacts of the ﬁshery on non-target species in the food web (Péron et al., 2016).
This requires an understanding of the interactions between ﬁsheries, targeted species, and other species in
the food web (Constable et al., 2000).
The plateau is a breeding site and foraging location for a wide range of marine animals (Thiers et al., 2017).
Due to the proximity of the ﬁshery to foraging grounds, the food web has been observed interacting with the
ﬁshery . For example, sperm whales and to a lesser extent, killer whales have been observed taking toothﬁsh
from the longlines on the plateau (Tixier et al., 2010; Labadie et al., 2018). Seabirds have been caught
on hooks of the longline and drowned as the hooks sink below the surface (Delord et al., 2010). Seabird
mortality through by-catch can have lasting impacts on breeding populations due to their long life cycles
(Delord et al., 2010, 2008). While the impacts on seabirds are now almost completely mitigated through
management measures, the role of targeted species in the food web is unknown yet important for ecosystem-
based management.
Observed population changes on the plateau have been linked to multiple factors. Growth and survival rates
of juvenile albatross species are thought to be sensitive to sea surface temperature (SST) (Jenouvrier et al.,
2018). Steady decreases in population size over the past 40 years have been observed for several species of
albatross on the Kerguelen Plateau while increases have been observed for others, most likely resulting from
multiple stressors such as climate change, ﬁshing and diseases (Weimerskirch et al., 2018). For example,
illegal ﬁsheries by-catch around the Kerguelen Islands was responsible for the observed decline in black-
browed albatrosses during the 2000s (Weimerskirch et al., 2018).
Prey availability as a result of environmental change can impact predator populations in many ways. A de-
cline in gentoo penguins on the Kerguelen Plateau from 1987-2004 was linked to reduced prey availability
as a result of competition, ﬁsheries, or environmental change (Lescroël and Bost, 2006). The breeding per-
formance of blue petrels decreased during years when warming anomalies reduced prey availability in their
foraging grounds (Guinet et al., 1998) and decreases in southern elephant seals were linked to reduced prey
availability as a result of environmental change (Guinet et al., 1999). As the effects of climate change increase
over the coming decades, greater impacts are likely . King penguin foraging range is expected to increase for
penguins living on the Kerguelen Islands, and decrease for penguins living on Heard and McDonald Island,
which may impact breeding success (Cristofari et al., 2018).
An improved understanding of food web dynamics on the plateau is crucial to understanding the combined
impacts of ﬁsheries and climate change on the ecosystem. Food web structure and function can be described in
terms of primary production, structure (e.g. relationships between functional groups), and energy utilisation
in the food web. These properties are unknown for the plateau and will be useful for addressing questions
regarding the current status, trends, and future scenarios for Southern Ocean ecosystems (Constable et al.,
2016).
Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) is a commonly-used software for developing food web models. The Ecopath
module of EwE describes the food web averaged over a known period and has been used to develop food web
models for many regions of the Southern Ocean (e.g. Hill et al. 2012; Pinkerton et al. 2010; Bradford-Grieve
24
Chapter 3
et al. 2003; Suprenand and Ainsworth 2017; Dahood et al. 2019). Ecopath uses fewer parameters than other
whole ecosystem models such as Atlantis (Fulton et al., 2004) and incorporates more of the ecosystem than
simplistic size-based models (such as NPZD models). Compared to minimum realistic models like GADGET
(Globally applicable Area Disaggregated General Ecosystem Toolbox) (Plagányi 2007 and references therein)
that are used to focus on important interactions with the species of interest (i.e. species targeted by the
ﬁshery), EwE is well-suited for exploring food web interactions with the ﬁshery in regions like the Kerguelen
Plateau where this knowledge is limited. EwE can also estimate missing parameters where data is lacking for
species or groups of organisms in the food web (Plagányi, 2007). Outputs from the model describe the food
web in terms of energy ﬂow and primary production that can be compared across models (Christensen et al.,
2008) and used in ecosystem-based risk assessments (Holsman et al., 2017).
Chapter 2 (Subramaniam et al., 2019) implemented a balanced version of the model presented in Pruvost
et al. (2005) describing the food web within the French EEZ surrounding the Kerguelen Islands. In this
chapter, we expand the domain of this model to include Heard and McDonald Islands (Fig 3.1). With this
extended model, we capture the entire biomass of the Kerguelen Plateau by re-structuring groups to better
understand energy ﬂow in the food web and incorporating ﬁsheries data from both French and Australian
ﬁsheries. The new structure of functional groups further separates predators into groups based on their diets
and behaviours, we also add more resolution to lower trophic level groups. We intentionally provide extensive
detail on model development and give a thorough analysis of model outputs based on best practice guides
(e.g. Heymans et al. 2016).
3.2 Methods
3.2.1 Model domain
Our model domain, deﬁned as the area between 60◦–80◦E and 45◦–56◦S, covers the Kerguelen Islands (north)
and Heard and McDonald Islands (south) as well as the extent of the French and Australian ﬁshing zones (Fig
3.1). Our model covers an area of approximately 1.7 million km 2 and aims to represent the food web over
a period of 10 years, however we have used data from earlier years for some species to provide a complete
representation of the food web.
3.2.2 Ecopath with Ecosim
This study is conﬁned to Ecopath, where organisms in the food web are organised into functional groups based
on similarities in diet and behaviour. Functional groups are described in terms of biomass, consumption and
production, which are then linked through diet composition. Ecopath uses a mass-balanced approach to
describe the food web and this is achieved through two master equations (Christensen and Walters, 2004).
The ﬁrst equation describes the production term within each functional groupi:

× DC ji + Ei + BAi + P

of the predator, Yi is total ﬁshery catch, Q
B j is the consumption to biomass ratio for the predator j, DC ji is
the proportion of group i in the diet of predator j, Ei is net migration rate (emigration - immigration), BAi

is biomass accumulation rate, EE is the ecotrophic efﬁciency (the proportion of the production that is used
within the system) and (1− EE ) is other mortality .
The second equation describes the consumption term for each functional group i:
Q
B i
× Bi = P
B i
× Bi + Ri + UC i
where Q
B× B is consumption, P
B× B is production, R is respiration and UC is unassimilated consumption.
This equation ensures energy balance within each group by requiring that the consumption of each functional
group is always less than or equal to its production. The output gives an EE value for each group. If EE is
greater than 1 then parameters are adjusted until the EE of each functional group is less than one. We used
Ecopath version 6.5 to develop our model, which does not include an option to automatically balance the
model. Therefore, we balanced our model by manually adjusting parameters, a common approach for model
development in Ecopath (Hill et al., 2012).
3.2.3 Data compilation
We expanded the model domain by updating the data and restructuring the functional groups from Chapter 2
(Subramaniam et al., 2019). Mammals were organised into the following functional groups in Chapter 2 (and
in Subramaniam et al. 2019): top predators (consisting of sperm whales and killer whales), baleen whales,
and a single group for seal and dolphin species. We updated the data for baleen whale species using recent
population estimates, which were only available for ﬁn whales and southern right whales (Edwards et al.,
2015; Bannister et al., 2015), therefore only these two species were kept in the baleen whales group (see
Appendix A for a more detailed discussion).
Sperm whales and killer whales were split into distinct groups to allow for the investigation of their separate
roles in the food web. Similarly , we split Antarctic fur seals and southern elephant seals into separate groups
due to their differing diets (see diet matrix below). A toothed whales group was added to the model to
represent other mammals foraging over the Kerguelen Plateau for which there are little data. This group
comprised of the Southern bottlenose whale and the hourglass dolphin. Seabirds were split into three groups;
penguins, albatrosses, and other seabirds, based on the length of time they spent away from the plateau and
their slight variations in diet.
We grouped cephalopods together in one group as their diets were similar (Chapter 2, Subramaniam et al.
2019). We followed the groupings in Chapter 2 (Subramaniam et al., 2019) for ﬁsh groups and referred to
ﬁsh surveys (Duhamel et al., 2014; Duhamel and Hautecoeur, 2009) to assign ﬁsh species to groups (see
Appendix A). Small mesopelagic ﬁsh in Chapter 2 (Subramaniam et al., 2019) included myctophids (lantern-
ﬁsh); however, we have placed myctophids in a separate group in our updated model to isolate the energy
pathways to and from myctophids. We also separated mackerel iceﬁsh (Champsocephalus gunnari) and Patag-
onian toothﬁsh (Dissostichus eleginoides) into separate groups as they are both targeted by the ﬁshery .
Zooplankton were organised into four groups based mostly on size. Macrozooplankton (> 20 mm in length)
were split into two groups based on a study by Hunt et al. (2011), which found that euphausiids were domi-
nant around Kerguelen Island and Heard Island. Therefore, euphausiid species were organised into one group
labelled "Krill" to track energy ﬂow to and from krill species. The next dominant groups from the study were
chaetognaths and amphipods, which were organised into a single group along with other macrozooplankton
due to low data resolution. Mesozooplankton (0.2–20 mm) studies report that copepods are the dominant
27
Chapter 3
group on the plateau, with remaining mesozooplankton largely representing larval stages of macrozooplank-
ton (Carlotti et al., 2015; Mayzaud et al., 2002b; Carlotti et al., 2008; Labat et al., 2002). As a consequence
we chose to put all mesozooplankton species into one group. Microzooplankton (2–200µm) (Christaki et al.,
2015) were included as a single group in the model.
Benthic taxa in Chapter 2 (or Subramaniam et al. 2019) were split into three groups and parameterised
using values from Okey and Pauly (1999). We compiled benthic taxa into one group for this model as their
diets were relatively similar. Bacteria were updated using data from a study investigating bacterial biomass
around the Kerguelen Plateau (Becquevort et al., 2000). The macroalgae group in this model represents the
dominant giant kelp species on the Kerguelen Plateau, Durvillea antarctica and Macrocystis pyrifera (Pruvost
et al., 2005). Biomass data were only available for Macrocystis pyrifera therefore we used this species as a
representative biomass for the group (Delille et al., 2009). Phytoplankton biomass was updated with a recent
satellite estimate of Chl a concentration on the plateau (Dragon et al. 2011, see Appendix A for calculations
and conversions).
Two detritus groups were speciﬁed in this model to separate material consumed by scavengers from material
decomposed by bacteria. "Carcass" represents non-living organic matter from animals that have died for
reasons other than predation (such as disease or starvation) as well as parts of animals that were not consumed
during predation (i.e. messy eating). "Detritus" represents the remaining non-living matter (such as faeces).
All groups are listed in Table 3.1. Uncertainty and data quality were accounted for using the pedigree index
in Ecopath (see Appendix A).

Biomass
We calculated biomass for whales, seals and seabirds by multiplying population estimates by the average
weight of each species. Weights for seals and whales were taken from Trites and Pauly 1998 and more recent
sources for other species (see Appendix A). Estimates were then weighted by the fraction of time spent on the
plateau. If a range of population estimates was reported, the average between the minimum and maximum
was used. Absolute biomass was converted to tonnes per square kilometre by dividing biomass over the model
area or survey area if provided. If a density was reported (as individuals per km−2), we multiplied this density
by species weight to estimate tonnes per square kilometre.
There are no population or biomass estimates for cephalopods on the Kerguelen Plateau, therefore we used
Ecopath to calculate cephalopod biomass by providing a value for ecotrophic efﬁciency (EE). Ecotrophic ef-
ﬁciencies are close to 1 for groups that are heavily preyed upon and values of 1 (or close to 1) have been
reported for cephalopod groups in balanced Ecopath models (e.g. Hill et al. 2012; Ballerini et al. 2014).
Studies of predator diets on the Kerguelen Plateau show that cephalopods are widely preyed upon in this
region (Cherel et al., 2005). Therefore, we retained the EE value of 0.95 used in Chapter 2 (Subramaniam
et al., 2019) that has also been used in other Antarctic Ecopath models (Pinkerton et al., 2010) to estimate
cephalopod biomass. Biomass estimates for ﬁsh groups were taken from random stratiﬁed trawl surveys
(RSTS), undertaken annually on the plateau, and used for large mesopelagic ﬁsh, other demersal ﬁsh and
large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh. Small mesopelagic ﬁsh species (including myctophids) were not represented in
these surveys. In lieu of a better estimate, we used an initial estimate from Lam and Pauly (2005) and Hill
30
Chapter 3
et al. (2012) for myctophids and small mesopelagic ﬁsh groups, respectively . Patagonian toothﬁsh were split
into juveniles and adults and described in the model as a multi-stanza group. Toothﬁsh caught during the
RSTS were under 75 cm, therefore we used this as a biomass estimate for juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh.
Biomass estimates for macrozooplankton were obtained by digitising Fig 2 in Hunt et al. (2011) and averag-
ing biomass over the year. Biomass was split between macrozooplankton groups using species composition
reported in the study . Hunt et al. (2011) reported that biomass consisted of 63% euphausiid species, with
the remaining biomass consisting of amphipods, chaetognaths, salps, gastropods, siphonophores, and others.
Biomasses for other zooplankton groups were often reported in units of carbon. Carbon biomass for meso-
zooplankton was taken from Carlotti et al. (2015) and converted to dry biomass using the conversion rate
supplied in the study then converted to wet biomass (see Appendix A). Microzooplankton carbon biomass was
taken from Christaki et al. (2015) and converted to wet biomass per km −2 (calculations and conversions in
Appendix A). Bacterial biomass was calculated using carbon biomasses reported in Becquevort et al. (2000).
An assessment of benthic biomass on Heard Island indicated an average wet weight biomass of 86 t km −2
(Welsford et al., 2014). We used this estimate as a starting value for zoobenthos in the model as it compares
to the initial value of 72 t km−2 used in Hill et al. (2012).
Macroalgal biomass was taken from a study by Delille et al. (2009) and divided over the area of the model do-
main. In situ observations and satellite data were used to estimate a range for phytoplankton biomass, these
are described in Appendix A. Biomass used to parameterise the model was calculated using average satellite
chlorophyll a concentration between winter and summer (Dragon et al., 2011). Detritus biomass was calcu-
lated using an empirical equation (see Appendix A) and an average primary production of 0.61 gC m −2 d−1
for the plateau (Uitz et al., 2009).
Production and consumption parameters
Consumption/biomass (Q/B) and production/biomass (P/B) parameters for predators and ﬁsh groups were
adopted from other studies. Production and consumption rates for zooplankton and primary production were
calculated using studies reporting growth and consumption or reported production/consumption (P/Q) rates.
In addition to consumption and production parameters, the multi-stanza grouping assumes that body growth
of the species follows a von Bertalanffy growth curve (Christensen and Walters, 2004). A growth coefﬁcient
(K) of 0.12 for Patagonian toothﬁsh was taken from Ashford et al. (2005).
Non-living material was allocated to each of the detritus groups by specifying proportions using the detritus
fate tab in Ecopath. Following the Ecopath model for the Ross Sea (Pinkerton et al., 2010), unassimilated
consumption (UC) was directed to detritus fate, and other mortality (OM) was directed to the carcass group.
Fractions used to describe UC and OM were taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010) and are speciﬁed in Table 3.2.
Diet compositions were taken from diet studies that were speciﬁc to the plateau where possible or taken from
other sub-Antarctic models (Table 3.4). All data sources and calculations are presented in the Appendix A.
Fisheries catch and by-catch
Fisheries catch and by-catch information was taken from CCAMLR annual ﬁshery reports ( https://www.
ccamlr.org/). An average catch over 10 years (2005-2015) was used as long-term mean catch. Catch was
split by gear type and location, calculated in tonnes and divided over the area of the model domain (Table

By-catch was incorporated into the model in the same way as ﬁsheries catch (entered as t km−2, Christensen
et al. 2008). Fish species that were most commonly reported in by-catch were macrourid species, blue an-
timora (Antimora rostrata), rajid species ( Bathyraja murrayi, Bathyraja eatonii, Bathyraja irrasa), unicorn
iceﬁsh (Channichthys rhinoceratus) and grey rockcod (Lepidonotothen squamifrons). Seabird species reported
in the by-catch of the Kerguelen toothﬁsh ﬁshery were the white-chinned petrel, grey petrel, and northern
giant petrel. Seabird species caught as by-catch in the Heard Island toothﬁsh ﬁshery were the cape petrel,
white-chinned petrel and the black-browed albatross. White-chinned petrels and black-browed albatrosses
were also caught as by-catch in the mackerel iceﬁsh ﬁshery along with grey-headed albatrosses. By-catch was
split between other demersal ﬁsh and large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh groups in the model. Seabird species were
omitted due to the very low biomasses caught in by-catch.

3.2.4 Ecosystem properties and network analysis
Summary statistics built into Ecopath were used to describe the properties of our model and compare them
to the properties of other Antarctic food web models. Ecopath calculates the trophic level for each functional
group and calculates energy ﬂow through aggregated trophic levels using methods cited in Lindeman (1942)
and Ulanowicz (1995). We used the ’Lindeman spine’ (Lindeman, 1942) to describe energy ﬂow from one
aggregated trophic level to another, and used transfer efﬁciencies to describe the proportion of the ﬂow con-
sumed or exported at the next trophic level. We show the distribution of biomass of each functional group
over the aggregated trophic levels using proportional ﬂows to each trophic level by the group (Christensen
et al., 2008). We also provide a rough indication of resource consumption for each functional group using the
primary production required (PPR) function. All paths from trophic level I to the functional group are identi-
ﬁed and raised to primary production equivalents using the product of the catch, the consumption/production
ratio of each path element multiplied by the proportion the next element of the path contributes to the diet
of the given path element (see Christensen et al. 2008).
Mixed trophic impacts
The mixed trophic impacts tool was used to calculate the net impact of one functional group on another
through the following equation:
M T Ii,j = DCi,j− FC i,j
where the net impact of group i on group j (M T Ii,j) is given by the difference between the fraction of the
prey i in the diet of the predator j (DCi,j) and the fraction of total consumption of i used by predator j (FC i,j)
(Libralato et al., 2006; Christensen et al., 2008). This is done for each functional group, producing a matrix
of net impacts.
Keystoneness
The keystoneness tool was used to identify the functional groups that have a small biomass but a strong
inﬂuence on other groups in the food web. This was done in Ecopath by ﬁrst estimating the interaction
strength resulting from a change to the biomass of the functional group through the mixed trophic impacts
and then relating this to biomass proportion (Libralato et al., 2006).
An index of keystoneness is assigned to each functional group through the following equation:
KSi = log[ϵi(1−ρi)]
whereϵ is the sum of the relative trophic interactions for groupi andρ is the proportional biomass for group
i.
3.3 Results
3.3.1 Balancing the model
Ecotrophic efﬁciencies were initially greater than 1 for ﬁsh and zooplankton groups. Biomass parameters
for ﬁsh groups were adjusted to achieve mass-balance as consumption (Q /B) and production (P /B) values
35
Chapter 3
were largely attained from empirical methods or values used in other models. Biomasses and P /Q ratios for
zooplankton groups were adjusted to achieve mass-balance.
We increased biomass for mackerel iceﬁsh (by 117%), large mesopelagic ﬁsh (31%), other demersal ﬁsh
(12%), large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh (146%), microzooplankton (73%) and bacteria (59%). We decreased
biomass for myctophids (2%), small mesopelagic ﬁsh (9%), krill (32%), other macrozooplankton (10%),
mesozooplankton (29%), zoobenthos (63%), and phytoplankton (15%). We increased P/B for phytoplankton
and microzooplankton (48% and 42%, respectively) and increased P /B and Q /B for myctophids (by 100%
and 31%, respectively) and bacteria (69% for both). Additionally , we increased P/Q values for krill (158%),
mesozooplankton (171%), microzooplankton (49%) and decreased P/Q for other macrozooplankton (64%).
The resulting balanced parameters are presented in Table 3.5.
Ecotrophic efﬁciencies were zero for whale groups, and close to zero for seals and penguins (Table 3.5).
Higher EEs were calculated for albatrosses and other seabirds. EEs were high for ﬁsh and zooplankton groups,
including mackerel iceﬁsh. Juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh EE was lower than other ﬁsh groups but higher than
adult Patagonian toothﬁsh.
Along with EE values, Ecopath calculates the trophic level of each functional group in the balanced model us-
ing the relative diet contributions of their prey . Producers are assigned to the ﬁrst trophic level and consumers
are assigned to a trophic level of 1 plus the weighted average of the trophic level of their prey (Christensen
et al., 2008). Trophic levels for our balanced model are presented in Table 3.5 and visually presented in
Fig 3.2. In our balanced model, sperm whales occupy the highest trophic level at 5.7, followed by southern
elephant seals (5.4), killer whales (5.3), toothed whales and dolphins (5.3), albatrosses and adult Patagonian
toothﬁsh (5.1), juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh (5), Antarctic fur seals and cephalopods (4.8), penguins (4.5)
and other seabirds (4.2). Baleen whales and myctophids have the same trophic level (3.8), small mesopelagic
ﬁsh, other demersal ﬁsh, krill, and zoobenthos also occupy trophic level 3 (3.9, 3.6, 3.1, and 3.1, respectively).
A trophic level of 2 is calculated for other macrozooplankton, mesozooplankton, microzooplankton, and bac-
teria.
3.3.2 Ecosystem properties
Summary statistics were used to describe the maturity and size of the food web in terms of energy ﬂow that
was compared across Ecopath models (Table 3.6). We compared our results to other published Antarctic
models (Hill et al., 2012; Gurney et al., 2014; Cheung and Pitcher, 2005; Subramaniam et al., 2019; Bre-
desen, 2003; Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana, 2008; Hoover, 2012; Erfan and Pitcher, 2005) using summary
statistics published in Gurney et al. (2014) and Chapter 2 (Subramaniam et al., 2019) (Fig 3.3). Total system
throughput describes the size of the system via the sum of consumption, exports, respiration, and ﬂows to
detritus. On the Kerguelen Plateau, consumption and respiration contributed most to total system throughput
and was higher than other Antarctic food web models (Fig 3.3). Although consumption and respiration were
relatively high, total system throughput of ∼22,000 t km−2 y−1 for the Kerguelen Plateau was in the range
of values found for Antarctic models. Total biomass for this system was ∼170 t km−2, which is in the upper
range of total biomasses observed for other Antarctic models. Mean trophic level of ﬁsheries catch was 5.03,
indicating that ﬁshed species were situated towards the top of the food web, and gross efﬁciency of the catch
indicated the fraction of primary production required to sustain this catch is comparatively low in our model
(Table 3.6).
System maturity was described using the following statistics and /or ratios calculated by the model: total

primary production/total respiration, total primary production/total biomass, net system production, and to-
tal biomass/total throughput. A system still in development has a total primary production /total respiration
value greater than or less than 1, high total primary production /total biomass, high net system production,
and low total biomass/total throughput (Odum, 1969). Summary statistics for our model indicated that the
ecosystem on the Kerguelen Plateau is still developing. Total primary production /total biomass was greater
than 1 in this model (1.18), and total primary production /total biomass was moderate (27.18), considering
the highest value is close to 100 t km−2 (Fig 3.3). Net system production represents the difference between
total primary production and respiration and was high for the Kerguelen Plateau; however, it appears mod-
erate when compared to other sub-Antarctic models. Total biomass /total throughput was low, especially in
comparison to other Antarctic models.

3.3.3 Network analysis
Consumption, predation, exports/catches, respiration, and ﬂows to detritus for each aggregated trophic level
are presented in the Lindeman spine (Fig 3.4). We present ﬂows up to trophic level VI as the majority of
total ﬂows were covered within these levels. Trophic ﬂow from primary producers and detritus to trophic
level II was 2240 t km−2 and 5588 t km−2, respectively . This represents a herbivory to detritivory ratio of
0.4, indicating detritivory is important in the model. Trophic levels II and III contribute most to the high
consumption and respiration ﬂows seen in Table 3.6, biomass is also highest in these trophic levels. Total
system throughput (TST %), representing the size of each aggregated trophic level, was highest in detritus
and trophic level II. Transfer efﬁciency (TE in Fig 3.4) describes the proportion of total ﬂows at each trophic
level that is either exported or consumed at the next trophic level (Bradford-Grieve et al., 2003). Transfer
efﬁciency decreased as trophic level increased, with most ﬂow transferred between trophic levels II and III.
