Transfer efﬁciencies presented in Table 3.7 further break down proportions in Fig 3.4 by resolving efﬁciencies
coming from primary producers (phytoplankton and macroalgae) and detritus. Total transfer efﬁciency for
aggregated trophic levels II-IV was 13.91% from primary producers only , while transfer efﬁciency was 19.89%
for primary producers when detritus was included. Total ﬂow originating from detritus was higher than ﬂows
originating from primary producers, conﬁrming that detritivory is important in the ﬂow of energy from trophic
level I to II. Transfer from primary producers was highest in level III (18.09%), with transfer efﬁciencies in
level II, V , VI following in efﬁciency (13.76%, 13.22%, and 13.35%, respectively). Transfer from detritus was
highest in II and IV (28.06% and 19.86%, respectively).
Transfer efﬁciencies corresponded with the distribution of biomass by aggregated trophic level. Figure 3.5

and macroalgae are only present in the ﬁrst trophic level. More than half of bacteria, microzooplankton,
and other macrozooplankton biomass occurs in the second trophic level. Most of the predator biomasses are
distributed in trophic levels III, IV , and V .

Direct and indirect interactions in the model are presented in the mixed trophic impacts plot by showing the
overall positive or negative impact of an increase in the biomass of the impacting group on the impacted
group (Fig 3.6). Cephalopods had the most impacts on other functional groups in the model. Cephalopods
negatively impacted some ﬁsh groups (juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh, mackerel iceﬁsh, large mesopelagic ﬁsh,
41
Chapter 3
and other demersal ﬁsh) and positively impacted the predators that consume a lot of cephalopods (toothed
whales and dolphins, sperm whales, southern elephant seals and albatrosses). Similarly , myctophids posi-
tively impacted marine mammals, most notably Antarctic fur seals, and negatively impacted other seabirds
and ﬁsh groups in similar trophic levels, especially mackerel iceﬁsh, which had a ﬂow-on impact to the ﬁsh-
ery . The opposite impact is observed for increased krill biomass on mackerel iceﬁsh. Increased krill biomass
also had a positive impact on penguins and other seabirds. An increase in the biomass of killer whales nega-
tively impacted other predators (Antarctic fur seal, southern elephant seal, penguins, albatrosses, and other
seabirds). This is expected as they are the only group preying on these groups in the model. There was no
overall impact from sperm whales on Patagonian toothﬁsh or the ﬁshery despite having a higher biomass than
killer whales and a larger allocation of toothﬁsh in their diet in the model. Southern elephant seals had a
negative impact on large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh, with ﬂow-on impacts to the
ﬁshery . An increase in ﬁsheries catch in the Kerguelen longline ﬁshery had a negative impact on both adult
Patagonian toothﬁsh and on other ﬁsheries in the model, however, by-catch had no impact on by-catch groups
(large mesopelagic ﬁsh and large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh).

3.3.4 Energy pathways
Ecopath calculates the percentage of primary production required (PPR) for consumption by each group as an
indication of resource consumption in the food web. Killer whales were the highest trophic level consumer,
requiring a large percentage of primary production for consumption (Fig 3.8). Cephalopods and myctophids
also required a higher percentage of primary production for consumption compared to other functional groups
43
Chapter 3
in similar trophic levels. Other predators with high PPR were southern elephant seals and penguins. Some ﬁsh
groups also had higher PPRs than others (juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh, mackerel iceﬁsh, small mesopelagic
ﬁsh and large mesopelagic ﬁsh). These groups corresponded with the keystoneness index. Functional groups
with a keystoneness index close to or greater than zero were considered key groups in the model. Killer
whales, cephalopods, and myctophids were identiﬁed as key functional groups (blue points in Fig 3.9).

3.4 Discussion
In this study we present the ﬁrst quantitative description of the food web on the northern Kerguelen Plateau.
Our results revealed key features of ecosystem structure and function, including high net system production
and the likely important roles for killer whales, cephalopods and myctophids in this system. The inclusion of
ﬁsheries catch and by-catch showed no impact of by-catch on the food web. However, interactions between
species targeted by the ﬁshery and other species (krill, myctophids, cephalopods, and southern elephant seals)
had ﬂow-on effects to the ﬁshery . Ecopath does not resolve ontological progression or size-based predator-prey
relationships. Such relationships are better resolved using size-spectrum models. However our model does
resolve the differences in juvenile and adult biomasses and trophic levels for Patagonian toothﬁsh, because
of the ecological and economic importance of the species as well as its large maximum size.
3.4.1 Model evaluation
Changes made to the biomasses of groups to balance the model were within the range calculated during
data compilation (see Appendix A) or reported in the literature. Ecopath estimated a biomass of 0.29 t
km−2 for cephalopods. This was higher than cephalopod biomasses in food web models for South Georgia
and the Prince Edward Islands (Hill et al., 2012; Gurney et al., 2014), but lower than estimates for the
Kerguelen Islands and the Antarctic Peninsula (Subramaniam et al. 2019; Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana
2008) indicating our estimate is within the range of estimates used for sub-Antarctic and Antarctic food
webs. Our lower biomass value compared to the Kerguelen Islands model is most likely due to the updated
P/B and Q/B values used to parameterise the group.
Balanced biomass for mackerel iceﬁsh was much higher than the initial value used to parameterise the model.
This discrepancy was also found in the Ecopath model for South Georgia (Hill et al., 2012). Random stratiﬁed
trawl surveys were used to calculate the input biomass per unit area for mackerel iceﬁsh in our model (see
Appendix A). A recent study investigated the discrepancy in biomass between observations and the South
Georgia model (Fallon et al., 2016). Findings show that biases in trawl surveys could arise from unequal
selection of sizes or due to the depth at which trawl surveys operate and suggest future estimates of abundance
should be derived from acoustic data (Fallon et al., 2016).
Our balanced biomass estimate (per unit area) for large mesopelagic ﬁsh was similar to medium pelagic
ﬁsh biomass and lower than large pelagic ﬁsh biomass estimated for the Antarctic Peninsula model, both
of which were calculated using Ecopath (Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana, 2008). However, our value was
higher than large pelagic ﬁsh biomass used for the Prince Edwards Islands model (estimated using another
Ecopath model) (Gurney et al., 2014) and for the Kerguelen Island model (estimated in Ecopath in chapter 2,
Subramaniam et al. 2019). Balanced biomass (per unit area) for other demersal ﬁsh was higher than estimates
for the Prince Edwards Islands (calculated using observations) and the Kerguelen Island (estimated using
Ecopath). However, our value was lower than the value for small demersal ﬁsh in the Ecopath model for the
Antarctic Peninsula, who used Ecopath to estimate biomass for this group (Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana,
2008). Balanced biomass for large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh was similar to large demersal ﬁsh biomass in
Cornejo-Donoso and Antezana (2008). These comparisons indicate that our balanced biomass values for
these groups are in the range of values estimated for similar sub-Antarctic and Antarctic models. Our values
could be higher than the Prince Edwards Islands model and the Kerguelen Island model because our model
domain is larger than these two regions, and includes the productive Heard Island ecosystem.
46
Chapter 3
Balanced biomass for Patagonian toothﬁsh was higher than in the Prince Edwards Islands model (Gurney
et al., 2014) and South Georgia model (Hill et al., 2012), where toothﬁsh were included in the model as
individual functional groups. This could be because only juvenile toothﬁsh were included in the South Georgia
model and the Prince Edwards Islands is a smaller ecosystem (total biomass of 42 t km −2 compared to our
170 t km−2). Our biomass estimate comes from ﬁsh surveys conducted on the plateau, with an upper limit of
1.4 t km−2 for Heard Island (Nowara et al. 2017, see Appendix A). Therefore, our balanced value is within the
range of biomasses calculated for our model. Additionally , the toothﬁsh population on the Kerguelen Plateau
is one of the largest worldwide (Péron et al., 2016).
Ecotrophic efﬁciencies (EEs) for each functional group were similar to those published in other sub-Antarctic
and Antarctic Ecopath models. High EEs for ﬁsh and zooplankton groups were expected as they are heavily
featured in predator diets. EE values for commercially targeted ﬁsh species show that mackerel iceﬁsh are
heavily consumed in the food web while Patagonian toothﬁsh are moderately consumed. Predator groups
had EE values close to zero, which is expected as they are not preyed upon by other groups (Cornejo-Donoso
and Antezana, 2008). Albatrosses had higher EE values compared to other predator groups. This result is
similar to calculated EEs for albatross groups in the South Georgia model. The EE value for phytoplankton is
lower than values reported in other balanced Ecopath models but close to the EE expected for regions such
as the Kerguelen Plateau where seasonal phytoplankton blooms occur (Christensen et al., 2008).
3.4.2 Energy transfer and major linkages
Summary statistics for our balanced model indicate that the food web exhibits similar properties to other
sub-Antarctic and Antarctic models. High consumption and respiratory ﬂows reported in trophic level II were
likely due to the inclusion of bacteria and microzooplankton in the model and their high consumption rates
compared to other functional groups.
Energy transfer from detritus to trophic level II was greater than energy transfer from primary producers,
because bacteria feed exclusively on detritus and only occur in trophic level II. Our model suggests primary
production is more important further up the food web. Bacteria included in other Ecopath models has been
shown to dominate ﬂow through trophic levels (Christensen and Pauly, 1993). Few models have attempted to
quantify detrital pathways in the food web (Pinkerton et al., 2010) and when the microbial loop was included
in these models, more energy entered the food web through detrital pathways. Therefore we consider our
transfer from detritus appropriate for the Kerguelen Plateau (Heymans et al., 2016).
Key functional groups in the food web were killer whales, cephalopods, and myctophids. These groups,
especially killer whales and cephalopods, had the biggest impact on other groups in the model and had the
highest PPR values, indicating a high consumption of resources. Killer whales exhibited a strong top-down
effect on trophic interactions in the model, probably because they consumed more functional groups that had
high PPR values. Toothed whales and dolphins did not show a similar top-down effect despite also preying
on groups that had high PPR values, most notably cephalopods. killer whales in the model could be showing
this top-down effect because they are able to acquire energy through more pathways than toothed whales
and dolphins (i.e. by preying on other predators).
Killer whales are natural predators of Patagonian toothﬁsh and are present on the plateau in small numbers.
Commercial ﬁshing of toothﬁsh on the nearby Crozet Islands has modiﬁed the behaviour of killer whales
and sperm whales to consume toothﬁsh caught on the longlines and this behaviour is known as depredation
(Tixier et al., 2019; Labadie et al., 2018; Gasco et al., 2015). Depredation has not been frequently reported
47
Chapter 3
on the Kerguelen Plateau, however, if commercial ﬁshing were to attract more killer whales to the area, our
model suggests that killer whales could exert a strong top-down effect on the food web.
Cephalopods required the most primary production for consumption apart from zooplankton and bacteria.
The negative impacts of cephalopods on ﬁsh groups and positive impacts on most predators showed that
cephalopods were a dominant linkage between predators and prey in our food web model. Cephalopods had
a negative impact on Antarctic fur seals, possibly because they share prey . Myctophids had the second largest
PPR after cephalopods and were also seen to have positive impacts on most predators. Myctophids share prey
with other seabirds, which could explain the negative impact seen by myctophids on other seabirds in the
model. The positive impact of myctophids on Antarctic fur seals is expected as these ﬁsh make up over 90%
of the Antarctic fur seal diet.
Cephalopods and myctophids are often cited as dominant energy pathways or connecting groups between
lower and higher trophic levels in sub-Antarctic food webs (Murphy et al. 2016 and references therein).
Our model conﬁrms this as we used Kerguelen-speciﬁc dietary studies to populate our diet matrix for most
predators (seals and all seabirds). However, these studies could be biased as they only collect the remains
of undigested animal parts in gut contents and scats such as squid beaks (McInnes et al., 2016). Alternative
energy pathways in our model may be missing.
Gelatinous species such as jellyﬁsh and salps have been reported as alternative energy pathways in Southern
Ocean food webs (Murphy et al., 2016). However, unlike cephalopods and ﬁsh, these organisms are difﬁcult
to identify or quantify in gut contents and scats as they rarely leave behind identiﬁable material. Gelatinous
species (including ﬁsh larvae) were reported in small quantities in the diet of only two predator species on
the plateau (gentoo penguins, Lescroël et al. 2004 and Antarctic prions, Cherel et al. 2002b). DNA analysis of
black-browed albatross scats from the Kerguelen Islands suggests they may also be consuming small amounts
of gelatinous species (McInnes et al., 2016), but it is unknown how often predators are preying on gelatinous
species or if they target them during foraging trips. This is regarded as a minor issue and it is more likely that
prey organisms such as ﬁsh consume gelatinous species and this energy is transferred to predators through
ﬁsh consumption (Clarke et al., 2018). Additionally , as studies citing gelatinous species as an alternate energy
source were conducted in other regions of the Southern Ocean, more work is needed to clarify the importance
of this energy pathway for predators on the Kerguelen Plateau.
Our results could instead be biased by diet studies that have only sampled species during the summer or the
breeding season (e.g. Cherel et al. 2002c). However, many species are only present on the Kerguelen Plateau
during this time of the year and biomass in our study is weighted by time spent in the area to reﬂect this (see
methods and Cherel et al. 2005). Therefore, if our results were impacted by this bias, it may be negligible.
Krill and copepods are also cited as key energy pathways in sub-Antarctic regions (Murphy et al., 2016).
Krill and mesozooplankton (comprising mostly copepods) had the next highest keystone index following
myctophids in our model, indicating their likely importance in the food web. However, their keystoneness in
the food web could have been masked by our keystoneness result for cephalopods. Keystoneness is estimated
using relative trophic impact (based on mixed trophic impacts) and biomass (see keystoneness section). If the
biomass of the functional group is small, but their trophic impact is strong, the group is considered a keystone
group. It is possible that the importance of cephalopods in our model may be an artefact of our lack of data
and cephalopod biomass could be higher than what has been estimated by our model.
48
Chapter 3
3.4.3 Fishing and the food web
Our model shows that species targeted by the ﬁshery are situated towards the top of the food web and high EE
values for mackerel iceﬁsh and juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh indicate they are heavily consumed by predators.
Our results are similar to results from South Georgia and Prince Edwards Islands (Hill et al., 2012; Gurney
et al., 2014). Gross efﬁciency of the ﬁshery was low and lower than the global average of 0.0002, which is
expected of ﬁsheries that target species in higher trophic levels or that are under-exploited (Christensen et al.,
2008; Pauly et al., 2002).
Krill and myctophid interactions with mackerel iceﬁsh had ﬂow-on impacts to the ﬁshery . The positive impact
of krill biomass on mackerel iceﬁsh is expected as krill make up 70% of their diet. Shared prey most likely
explains the negative impact of myctophid biomass on mackerel iceﬁsh. Increases in mackerel iceﬁsh catch
however, did not negatively impact mackerel iceﬁsh or have any impact on the functional groups that eat or
are consumed by mackerel iceﬁsh. This could mean that the ﬁshery is currently not removing mackerel iceﬁsh
biomass at a level that is affecting food web dynamics.
Our model showed a separation of food web dynamics between juvenile and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh in the
food web. Overall impacts highlighted interactions between cephalopods and juvenile toothﬁsh, and southern
elephant seals and adult toothﬁsh. The negative impact of southern elephant seals on adult toothﬁsh and large
deep-sea demersal ﬁsh is most likely due to shared prey . Additionally , the top-down effect of killer whales
positively impacted all toothﬁsh ﬁsheries and is possibly due to the consumption of southern elephant seals.
Increases in Kerguelen ﬁshery catch had a negative impact on adult toothﬁsh biomass but this was not found
for the Heard Island ﬁsheries most likely because ﬁsheries catch in the Kerguelen EEZ is higher.
The integration of by-catch data into the model showed no effect of by-catch on non-targeted ﬁsh groups.
There was however, a competition effect between the Kerguelen longline ﬁshery and the Heard Island toothﬁsh
ﬁsheries. The likelihood of this impact in the real system depends on the connectivity of the stocks. Toothﬁsh
are reported to have a low annual migration rate between Kerguelen and Heard and McDonald (0.4%),
suggesting that stocks are weakly connected (CCAMLR, 2017; Burch et al., 2017) and that this competition
effect may not be reﬂective of the real world. While our model suggests that ﬁshing is not an issue, localised
over-ﬁshing, non-linear stock recruitment and spawning may still need to be evaluated. As these are spatial
issues, they cannot be examined with the current model. These issues will be evaluated in future work with
the model in Ecospace.
3.5 Conclusion
This study presents the ﬁrst quantitative description of the food web on the northern Kerguelen Plateau. Our
results indicate three key functional groups within the ecosystem: killer whales, cephalopods and myctophids.
These functional groups form an energy pathway that demonstrates a higher consumption of resources (PPR)
than other groups in similar trophic levels as well as negative trophic impacts on prey groups and positive
trophic impacts on predator groups. These groups also had the least available data, strongly suggesting more
data needs to be collected for these groups. This study has demonstrated important interactions in the food
web that would most beneﬁt from more focussed modelling studies in the future. Results from this study can
be used as a foundation to test scenarios for the effects of environmental change on food webs in the region
(Ecosim) and consider how ecosystem-based management might need to account for these effects. Future
work will include incorporating temporal and spatial variability into the model through Ecosim and Ecospace
49
Chapter 3
to understand the factors driving the food web and explore food web dynamics under different scenarios of
change (Chapter 3 and Chapter 4).
50
CHAPTER 4
Time-dynamic food web modelling to explore
environmental drivers of ecosystem change on
the Kerguelen Plateau
Abstract
Understanding the impacts of climate and ﬁshing on marine systems is important for ecosystem-based man-
agement in the Southern Ocean, but can be difﬁcult to evaluate due to patchy data in space and time. We
developed the ﬁrst time-dynamic food web model for the Kerguelen Plateau using Ecopath with Ecosim to
explore likely drivers of change in this relatively data-poor region. The Kerguelen Plateau is located at the
centre of intersecting frontal systems and is inhabited by one of the largest populations of the commercially
important Patagonian toothﬁsh. We used this model to evaluate the environmental and human drivers of
food web dynamics in the region by calibrating it with French and Australian ﬁsheries data from 1997–2018
and biomass data for the period 1986–2018. Fishing was not identiﬁed as a driver of food web dynamics
within this model, which could indicate that current management strategies are sustainable. A correlation
analysis with environmental parameters likely to drive food web dynamics (sea surface temperature, zonal
wind, Southern Annular Mode and chlorophyll a concentration) highlighted cool sea surface temperature,
higher zonal wind speeds and negative phases of the Southern Annular Mode as important drivers of change,
particularly during the summer. As the Southern Ocean is predicted to warm and winds are expected to inten-
sify under future climate change, our study illustrates the importance of considering environmental change
in ecosystem management.
4.1 Introduction
The Kerguelen Plateau, located in the sub-Antarctic Indian Ocean (Fig 4.1), intersects with the frontal systems
of the Antarctic Circumpolar Current (ACC) (Park et al., 2014). Driven by strong westerly winds, the ACC
brings water of varying temperatures (0.5–3◦C) to the Kerguelen Plateau (Mintenbeck, 2017; Park et al.,
2014). Upwelling leads to annual phytoplankton blooms that support breeding and foraging predators (Thiers
et al., 2017) and the largest ﬁshery for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Brooks et al., 2016).
The plateau is divided into two regions: the northern region, denoted by the hatched area in Fig 4.1 is a
French Exclusive Economic zone (EEZ) while the southern region, denoted by the striped area in Fig 4.1, is
an EEZ for Australia. Direct ﬁshing for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Dissostichus eleginoides) in the French EEZ began
as a trawl ﬁshery in 1985 and longline ﬁshing began in 1991. The ﬁshery has been longline only since 2001.
The ﬁshery is active all year, except late summer (February–March) to avoid seabird mortality (Duhamel et al.,
2011). Australia also operates a commercial ﬁshery from May to November in the southern region. Fishing
Chapter 4
for Patagonian toothﬁsh in the Australian EEZ began as a trawl ﬁshery in 1997, longline ﬁshing began in
2003 and gradually replaced trawling. A trawl ﬁshery for mackerel iceﬁsh (Champsocephalus gunnari) was
initiated at the same time as the toothﬁsh and continues today (from December–November). Both EEZs are
located within the CCAMLR area and while Australian catch limits are agreed through consensus in CCAMLR
(Mintenbeck, 2017), French catch limits are decided unilaterally .
There is limited understanding regarding how changes to the environment may impact the food web and
commercial ﬁshing (Meredith et al., 2019; Rayfuse, 2018). Sea surface temperature (SST) can affect the
growth rate, population structure, prey availability and breeding performance of predators in this region
(e.g. Pardo et al. 2017; Guinet et al. 1998; Cristofari et al. 2018; Lescroël and Bost 2006). Other observations
reveal that strong westerly winds have improved foraging performance and breeding success of albatrosses
in regions adjacent to the Kerguelen Plateau (Weimerskirch et al., 2012). SST and wind on the Kerguelen
Plateau have been linked to large-scale climate processes such as the Southern Annular Mode (SAM) (e.g.
Lovenduski and Gruber 2005). The SAM index is an important climate driver in the Southern Ocean and
is dictated by the position of strong westerly winds (Bost et al., 2015). However, the SAM has not yet been
related to food web dynamics. Positive SAM phases indicate a contraction of the winds poleward and negative
phases indicate an expansion of the wind belt towards the equator (Rogers et al., 2020). This strong band of
wind affects SST and oceanographic processes on the Kerguelen Plateau via upwelling or downwelling (Su
et al., 2020), inﬂuencing food web interactions.
Patagonian toothﬁsh are a slow growing, long-lived species, which makes them vulnerable to over-ﬁshing.
Recruitment is inﬂuenced by the environment (such as SST) making them susceptible to future climate change
(Mintenbeck, 2017). Correlating food web dynamics with climate requires consistent observations, which
is difﬁcult on the Kerguelen Plateau due to its remote location and harsh environmental conditions. Food
web models are a useful medium for exploring potential climatic impacts on ecosystems and for informing
ecosystem-based management practices (Fulton et al., 2019).
Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE, Christensen and Walters 2004) is a useful tool to explore the combined envi-
ronmental and human drivers of food web dynamics that are important for ecosystem-based management.
EwE is suited to data-limited regions such as the Kerguelen Plateau as it estimates values where there are
gaps in time-series data and uses food web interactions to generate trends for other functional groups where
data are absent. Furthermore, this method can identify sensitive trophic interactions and approximates an
environmental trend that can be assessed for correlation with climate trends.
In this study , we use EwE to calibrate the Ecopath model from Chapter 3 (Subramaniam et al., 2020a) with
time-series data to investigate environmental and human impacts on the Kerguelen Plateau food web.
4.2 Methods
4.2.1 Ecosim
EwE is a commonly-used software for developing food web models. The Ecopath module of EwE provides
a snapshot of the food web through two master equations that balance the energy input and output of the
food web (see Chapter 3, Subramaniam et al. 2020b). The balanced Ecopath model can then be calibrated
to time-series data in Ecosim.

Figure 4.1. Map of the Kerguelen Plateau, model domain (60 ◦–80◦E, 45◦–56◦S) is indicated by the white
box, the hatched area indicates the French EEZ, striped area indicates the Australian EEZ. Front positions and
boundaries of the Antarctic Circumpolar Current (ACC) as described in Park et al. (2014), bathymetry data
are from https://www.gebco.net. NB – northern boundary of the ACC, SAF – subantarctic front, PF –
polar front, SACCF – southern ACC front, SB – southern boundary of the ACC.
Biomass dynamics in Ecosim are described through a series of coupled simultaneous equations:
δBi
δt = gi
∑
j
Q ji−
∑
j
Qi j + Ii− (Mi + Fi + ei)× Bi
where δBi
δt represents the growth rate of group i during the time period t, gi is net growth efﬁciency (pro-
duction/consumption), Fi is ﬁshing mortality rate, Mi is other mortality rate, ei is emigration rate, Ii is
immigration rate and Bi is biomass. The consumption rates Qi j and Q ji are calculated based on a simple
"foraging arena" model while biomasses (Bi) are split into components that are vulnerable and invulnerable
to predation.
The performance of the model is assessed by calculating the weighted sum of squares (SS) between the log
observed data and model output (Christensen et al., 2008).

4.2.2 Ecosim input and calibration
We used the balanced Ecopath model described in Chapter 3 (Subramaniam et al., 2020b) as the baseline
Ecosim model. The model is comprised of 28 functional groups, organised based on similar diets and life
cycles. A visual representation of this model illustrates the structure of the food web and associated trophic
levels (Fig 4.2) balanced model parameters are provided in Tables 3.5 and 3.4. As the Kerguelen Plateau
is a data-poor system, Chapter 3 (Subramaniam et al., 2020b) provided an analysis of food web dynamics
averaged over a period of ten years, but also used data dating back to the late 1980s for some species.
Therefore, this model can be seen as a representation of the mean state of the ecosystem from the late 1980s
to 2018. A similar approach has been used for Ecosim models in other regions of the Southern Ocean (e.g.
Dahood et al. 2019).

Trawl surveys on the Kerguelen Plateau have shown large ﬂuctuations in the biomass of some ﬁsh species over
this time period. However, it is unclear if these ﬂuctuations have affected food web dynamics (e.g.Notothenia
rossii, Duhamel et al. 2019). Other observational studies in this region suggest no major directional change
over the time period considered in this model (e.g. Weimerskirch et al. 2018; Authier et al. 2011). Based on
this information, the aims of our study were to investigate patterns of variability between the environment
and the food web.
We used time-series data that were available and of sufﬁcient length to calibrate the model. We used popula-
54
Chapter 4
tion trends for southern right whales (Bannister et al., 2015), southern elephant seals (Authier et al., 2011),
black-browed albatrosses (Weimerskirch et al., 2018), and Patagonian toothﬁsh and mackerel iceﬁsh ﬁsheries
data to parameterise the model. A full description of these data is presented in Table B.1.
In the model, juvenile and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh were partitioned as having a length less than or greater
than 75 cm based on previous studies (Collins et al., 2007; Pruvost et al., 2005). Although juveniles may
actually mature later in life on the Kerguelen Plateau (Yates et al., 2018), we have chosen to remain consistent
with the commonly-used size separation for juveniles. Length-frequency analysis in ﬁshery reports (CCAMLR,
2018a,b) and minimum legal catch limits (60 cm) indicate that toothﬁsh under 75 cm are caught during the
ﬁshing season but it is unclear how much of the catch is less than 75 cm. Consultation with ﬁsheries scientists
was used to inform an estimated allocation of catch to juveniles.
Trawl ﬁshing for Patagonian toothﬁsh catches mostly immature ﬁsh while longline ﬁshing catches mostly
mature ﬁsh. To account for this difference in selection, time-series of nominal catch per unit effort (CPUE,
kg per hook) and total catch for each season was allocated 28% to juveniles and the remainder to adults for
longlines and trawl CPUE and catch was allocated to juveniles (Philippe Ziegler, Australian Antarctic Division,
pers. comm). Fishing mortality was used to drive ﬁshing activity in Ecosim using model estimated biomass
for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Table B.1), this was calculated as:
Fishing mortality = Catch
Biomass
Calibration of the model with time-series data was performed using the step-wise ﬁtting procedure described
in the EwE best practices guideline (Heymans et al., 2016). Previously , step-wise ﬁtting was done manually
however, an automated plug-in of this procedure was developed and recently implemented into the EwE
software (Scott et al., 2016). We opted to use the automated plug-in to calibrate the model. The step-
wise ﬁtting procedure adjusts vulnerabilities and a primary production anomaly (PP anomaly) for a set of
scenarios (see Fig 4.3). These parameters are estimated by reducing the sum of squares difference between
the predicted and observed data via an optimisation search routine (Scott et al., 2016). For the PP anomaly ,
this routine produces a trend that can be correlated with environmental observations (See Appendix B).
The vulnerability parameter describes interactions between predators and prey in the model. A value in the
range 1–100 is attributed to each interaction to dictate how consumption is inﬂuenced by changes in predator
and prey biomass. Values close to 1 indicate bottom-up control while values close to 100 indicate that top-
down inﬂuences dominate (Christensen et al., 2008). The PP anomaly parameter represents (non-modelled)
bottom-up control on the ecosystem (Scott et al., 2016), and typically represents some environmental inﬂu-
ence on phytoplankton. For each version of the model there were a possible 172 trophic interactions that
could be tuned to calibrate the model. We found that the maximum number of interactions that would elicit
a noticeable change in the sum of squares was 30 interactions (or vulnerabilities). Therefore, we restricted
our scenarios to a maximum of 30 vulnerabilities. Model ﬁt using step-wise ﬁtting is evaluated by calculating
the Akaike information criterion (AIC) and penalising for ﬁtting too many parameters based on the number
of time-series available resulting in a weighted AIC value (AICc) (Scott et al., 2016). We used a two-step
approach to select the best-ﬁtting model by ﬁrst selecting the models that were statistically the best ﬁt (had
lowest AIC values) then plotting and using a pattern oriented approach (Heymans et al., 2016; Kramer-Schadt
et al., 2007).
Our data compilation resulted in biomass trends and ﬁshing data for different periods of time. Biomass data
were mostly available for the entire time period, however, ﬁshing data were only available for shorter periods

blue and yellow) for each model conﬁguration in Table 4.1. Baseline scenarios (dark blue) do not include
ﬁsheries data. For scenarios including trophic interactions (blue) and PP anomalies (purple), each scenario
was run by increasing the number of vulnerabilities estimated for trophic interactions and PP anomaly spline
points by 5 until the maximum was reached (5, 10, 15...etc).
(Table B.1). Additionally , due to the evolving preferences for different types of ﬁshing gear when targeting
Patagonian toothﬁsh, there were three CPUE inputs that could be used as a proxy for toothﬁsh biomass trends
in the model. To cover potential biases resulting from data gaps, the step-wise ﬁtting procedure was performed
using four different conﬁgurations of the model (shown in Table 4.1). All four model conﬁgurations were very
similar, only differing in the length of time calibrated or CPUE and ﬁsheries data input.
For scenarios where CPUE inputs were averaged across French and Australian EEZs, only the trawl CPUE was
used to approximate the trend for juveniles and 72% of longline CPUE was used for the adults. Juvenile
catch and ﬁshing mortality were averaged over gears (trawl and 28% of the longline catch for the French and
Australian EEZ) and adult longline catch (72% of the longline catch for the French and Australian EEZ) and
ﬁshing mortality were averaged for these scenarios (Models 2 and 4, Table 4.1).
For the best ﬁtting model scenario, Monte Carlo simulations were run within EwE to test the sensitivity of
56
Chapter 4
the Ecosim model to the base Ecopath model input as an additional measure to validate our approach. The
Monte Carlo routine randomly selects starting biomass values for functional groups based on the coefﬁcient
of variation (CV) and calculates a new sum of squares based on the new initial values. The default CV in
Ecosim is±10% however, we tested ±20% Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass as the starting condition for our
Ecosim model. We varied toothﬁsh biomass as there is particular uncertainty about the biomass of this group
in the late 1980s (Duhamel et al., 2019; Duhamel and Hautecoeur, 2009).

We correlated the PP anomaly trend from the best ﬁtting model with chlorophyll a, wind, sea surface tem-
perature (SST) and the Southern Annular Mode index (SAM) taken from satellite data (sources provided in
Table B.2). Estimated PP anomaly and biomass trends from the model were given as monthly averages. To
match the model output, climate variables were also derived from satellite data as monthly averages using
the orange bounding box in Fig 4.1. We examined correlations between the model output from the best-
ﬁtting scenario, both monthly and annual averages, and anomalies of remotely sensed data. Environmental
variables were also correlated with each other to test the strength of each correlation.
Seasonal anomalies were calculated for summer (December to May of the following year) and winter (June to
November of the same year) by calculating the average over the time series then subtracting this value from
the seasonal (summer or winter) average for each year. Seasonal anomalies were compared to the summer
and winter average biomass for each group. For each set of anomalies (monthly , annual and seasonal), a
moving average was calculated in R using the "forecast" package with a 5-step moving window (Hyndman
57
Chapter 4
and Khandakar, 2008).
A Spearman’s rank correlation was used to test for signiﬁcance between model output and climate anomalies.
P-values were adjusted in R using the "p.test" function and the false discovery rate method (Jafari and Ansari-
Pour, 2019). We tested environmental correlations against the PP anomaly trend, phytoplankton, juvenile
and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh, other macrozooplankton and krill biomass. Due to the size and value of the
Patagonian toothﬁsh ﬁshery , and the large-scale removal of Patagonian toothﬁsh by ﬁsheries in both regions
of the Kerguelen Plateau, we focus on evaluating climate impacts on Patagonian toothﬁsh in this study .
Recruitment of Patagonian toothﬁsh is variable from year to year and most likely indirectly related to SST
(Mintenbeck, 2017; Yates et al., 2018). While there have been no observational studies examining recruit-
ment variability on the Kerguelen Plateau, studies from other regions of the Southern Ocean suggest this
could be important for predicting changes to toothﬁsh populations (e.g. Laptikhovsky and Brickle 2005).
The Patagonian toothﬁsh displays a long life cycle: adults spawn during austral winter (May-August), eggs
incubate for 3 months and hatch in pelagic waters. Juveniles inhabit pelagic waters until 13-15 months of
age where they start to settle in benthic habitats (< 500 m) (Péron et al., 2016). Adults are usually found at
depths greater than 600 m (Mintenbeck, 2017). Due to this long life cycle, climate impacts on recruitment
may be lagged.
Successful spawning during austral winter may only be observed in the juvenile population once they start to
become benthopelagic at 1+ years old (Belchier and Collins, 2008). Belchier and Collins (2008) found a 2 year
lag between summer SST and successful recruitment of juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh. Based on this ﬁnding
we tested for lagged effects up to 2 years between toothﬁsh groups and climate variables that did not show
a signiﬁcant correlation in the above analysis. We moved climate variables forward (equivalent to lagging
toothﬁsh biomass) by two, three and four seasons, corresponding to a 1, 1.5 and 2 year lag, respectively . We
performed a correlation analysis between the moved climate variables and toothﬁsh biomass trends.
4.3 Results
Model conﬁguration 3 demonstrated the best ﬁt of the four model conﬁgurations under consideration (model
3, Table 4.1). This was unsurprising as model 3 covered the longer time period, allowing for more data points
to ﬁt to. This model covered the entire time period and incorporated all three CPUE inputs for Patagonian
toothﬁsh. The best-ﬁtting scenarios from this model involved adjusting vulnerabilities and estimating PP
anomalies. All model ﬁts are presented in Table B.3.
Model 3 was improved when trophic effects and PP anomalies were included in the model. The best-ﬁtting
scenario set vulnerabilities for 5 trophic interactions (Table B.4) and 7 PP anomaly spline points (Fig B.2).
Importantly , the best-ﬁtting model did not include ﬁshing as a driver of food web dynamics. The model
reproduced the general trends for toothﬁsh groups, however, observed trends for baleen whales, southern
elephant seals, albatrosses and mackerel iceﬁsh were not well-represented (Fig B.1). Therefore, we sought
alternative vulnerabilities for use in the model.
We chose to use alternative vulnerabilities from model 1, which had the most comprehensive data coverage.
The best-ﬁtting scenario for model 1 was achieved when only trophic effects were included (see Table B.3).
Similarly to model 3, the best ﬁtting scenario for model 1 estimated vulnerabilities for 5 interactions. We
replaced the vulnerabilities shown in Table B.4 with those in Table 4.2 and ran the scenario again to calibrate
the model.
58
Chapter 4
The replacement vulnerabilities produced improved estimates for Patagonian toothﬁsh groups and mackerel
iceﬁsh compared to observations (Fig 4.4). We also compared both model ﬁts to a study representing pop-
ulation trends for predators (killer whales, toothed whales and dolphins, sperm whales, other seabirds and
Antarctic fur seals) using data collected by ﬁshery-observers (Gasco et al., 2019). Our comparison showed
that replacing the vulnerabilities provided a better representation of the observer data presented in Gasco
et al. (2019) (see Table B.5 in Appendix B).
Results from our sensitivity analysis indicated that while changing the initial conditions did result in increased
or decreased biomass throughout the time series, this occurred in a consistent manner (i.e. more biomass at
the start of the time series led to more biomass at the end of the time series). The pattern of biomass trends
for both toothﬁsh and other functional groups from this analysis show that our model was not sensitive to
initial conditions (Fig 4.5).

4.3.1 Correlation with environmental variables
Correlations between environmental variables showed that SST signiﬁcantly correlated with the SAM at the
annual timescale and summer season while zonal wind only signiﬁcantly correlated with the SAM during
summer. SST and zonal wind were not signiﬁcantly correlated with each other at any timescale (Table 4.3).
These differing correlations support our investigation of individual variables with modelled biomass trends.
Table 4.3. Correlation analysis between environmental anomalies (as moving averages). Bold text indicates
signiﬁcant correlations. P-values have been adjusted as described in methods.
Annual Wind SAM
SST r=-0.07, p=0.88 r=0.52, p=0.042
Wind r=0.43, p=0.1
Summer Wind SAM
SST r=0.05, p=0.88 r=0.84, p=8.6e-06
Wind r=0.72, p=8.1e-03
Winter Wind SAM
SST r=-0.1, p=0.88 r=-0.03, p=0.89
Wind r=0.26, p=0.43
SST , zonal wind and SAM were correlated with zooplankton and toothﬁsh biomass trends estimated by the
best-ﬁtting model. Correlations between estimated biomass and monthly anomalies for climate variables were
lower than those with annual and seasonal anomalies. Annual and seasonal anomalies are more likely to be
drivers of biological response over time than monthly anomalies, therefore we focus on these results for the
remainder of this study . Results from correlations with monthly anomalies are presented in Appendix B (Table
B.6).
PP anomaly and zooplankton biomass trends correlated negatively with annual SST and SAM, and positively
with annual zonal wind anomalies. Biomass increased during periods when SST was cooler than average
(1990-1994 and 2005-2007, Fig 4.6) and declined during periods when SST was warmer than average (e.g
1994-1999, Fig 4.6). SST did not appear to correlate with juvenile and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh, however
there may be a lagged effect (discussed below). Zonal wind correlated with all functional groups: estimated
biomass trends increased during periods where surface wind speed was faster than average, and decreased
during periods where surface wind speed was slower than average (Fig 4.7). PP anomaly and estimated
biomass trends for phytoplankton, macrozooplankton and krill increased during periods of negative SAM
anomaly , indicating that westerly winds were expanding further north than average (Fig 4.8).
Summer SST correlated better with PP anomaly , phytoplankton, macrozooplankton and krill biomass trends
(Table 4.4). Similar to annual SST , summer SST demonstrated a negative correlation with model variables.
Biomass peaked during periods when summer SST was cooler than average and declined during periods when
summer SST was warmer than average. Again, we found no correlation between summer SST and toothﬁsh
groups, which may be because of a lagged effect (discussed below). Estimated biomass trends were uncor-
related with winter SST (Table 4.4). Summer zonal wind correlated positively with biomass trends: biomass
increased during periods when summer surface wind speed was faster than average and decreased during
periods when summer surface wind was slower than average (Table 4.4). Winter wind positively correlated
with only adult Patagonian toothﬁsh (Table 4.4). Both summer and winter SAM exhibited a negative cor-
62
Chapter 4
relation with PP anomaly , phytoplankton, macrozooplankton and krill. In contrast, summer SAM correlated
positively with adult Patagonian toothﬁsh (Table 4.4).

We tested for lagged effects between juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh and summer SST , winter SST , winter zonal
wind, summer SAM and winter SAM as they did not show a signiﬁcant correlation in the above analysis. We
also tested for lagged effects of adult Patagonian toothﬁsh with summer SST , winter SST and winter SAM. We
present only signiﬁcant correlations in our results. Correlations appeared to be strongest at the three season
and four season lag. Our results show a negative correlation between lagged juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh
biomass and summer SST at all three seasonal lags, with the strongest correlation at the four season lag
(Fig 4.9). Lagged juvenile biomass also correlated negatively with summer and winter SAM with strongest
correlations at the three and four season lag. Lagged adult Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass correlated negatively
with summer SST at the four season lag and winter SAM at all three seasonal lags with the strongest at the
three season lag (Fig 4.9).

Chapter 4
4.4 Discussion
4.4.1 Model evaluation
Our analysis presents the ﬁrst attempt at calibrating food web dynamics on the Kerguelen Plateau with trends
in observed time-series. We acknowledge that this approach to model ﬁtting is not standard practice in the
EwE community . We used this approach to cover biases in data availability and focus our interpretations
on patterns of interannual variability instead of directional change. We implemented several models which
reproduced the observed data with varying success. The best-ﬁtting model reproduced observed biomass
trends for juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh, adult Patagonian toothﬁsh and mackerel iceﬁsh better than other
model versions and reproduced observed trends for groups with no input data (see Appendix B). The model
also partially recreated trends for southern elephant seals and albatrosses although it could not recreate the
trend for baleen whales.
Ecosim models are ﬁtted to the data by tuning parameters (vulnerabilities and PP anomaly trends) to show
which values could explain observed historical trends (Alexander et al., 2014). Consequently , the ability of
the model to ﬁt to observed data is improved with good quality data sets. In situations where data quality
is poor, the model may struggle to converge on an accurate solution. The inability of the preferred model to
completely represent observed trends for marine predators could be a result of data quality and the inability
to incorporate all possible drivers of change. Data on population trends for baleen whales on the Kerguelen
Plateau are lacking (Dréo et al., 2019). The data used to represent baleen whales was taken from an aerial
survey of Australian southern right whale population. Aerial surveys are known to have large biases therefore,
not much conﬁdence was placed in the data (Marsh and Sinclair, 1989). Additionally , this trend contributed
very little to the overall ﬁt of the model. Population trends of southern elephant seals may be related to their
life history and may be too complex to capture in aggregated models (Goedegebuure et al., 2018). Southern
elephant seals also forage outside of the Kerguelen Plateau (Delord et al., 2014). Declines in albatrosses
have been linked to demersal longline ﬁsheries, and particularly illegal, unregulated and unreported (IUU)
ﬁsheries by-catch (Michael et al., 2019), especially during the 1990s and early 2000s (Weimerskirch et al.,
2018). However, estimates of seabird mortality due to IUU are unavailable. Climatic conditions outside of
the Kerguelen Plateau during winter may further contribute to observed population trends for albatrosses as
they winter away from the Kerguelen Plateau (Weimerskirch et al., 2018).
Fishing was not identiﬁed as a driver of food web dynamics over this time period, indicating that in our model
representation, ﬁshing activity is currently sustainable. This is consistent with conclusions of sustainable
ﬁshing from a recent assessment of management of marine protected areas in the southern region of the
Kerguelen Plateau (Brooks et al., 2019). In addition to ﬁshing activity occurring much later into the time
series, more than one gear has operated during this time. Fishing activity occurred much later into the time
series therefore, it is possible that either the direct impacts of ﬁshing are too subtle for the model to capture or
the effects have not yet travelled through the food web. Moreover, we were unable to incorporate any ﬁshing
activity (trawl and longline) in the French EEZ prior to 2005, nor were we able to include IUU ﬁshing activity
due to data quality and/or data unavailability . Despite these limitations, the model was useful for evaluating
large-scale environmental drivers of food web dynamics.
68
Chapter 4
4.4.2 Environmental drivers of food web dynamics
Our results highlight environmental (or bottom-up control) as an important driver of food web dynamics on
the Kerguelen Plateau. Peaks in phytoplankton and zooplankton biomass correlated with negative annual
SST anomalies, increasing annual zonal wind speed and a negative SAM index. When seasonality was con-
sidered, phytoplankton and zooplankton biomass positively correlated with summers that exhibited cooler
temperatures and higher wind speeds. Their biomasses generally correlated with negative SAM anomalies
during summer and winter.
Cool surface waters combined with high wind speeds may enhance vertical mixing on the Kerguelen Plateau
as strong winds can increase the depth of the mixed layer and bring cold, nutrient-rich water into the euphotic
zone (Gille et al., 2014; Blain et al., 2007). This is not typical of negative SAM anomalies in the Southern
Ocean (see discussion below). Observations indicate that during austral summer, cold and well-mixed waters
are dominated by large phytoplankton on the Kerguelen Plateau (Quéguiner, 2013). The resulting zooplank-
ton composition is dominated by large copepods, leading to a short, energy-efﬁcient pathway for predators
(Venkataramana et al., 2019; Quéguiner, 2013). Results from our comparison between model output and
environmental variables are consistent with these observations.
Other factors such as negative wind stress curl can inﬂuence Ekman pumping and further enhance upwelling
(Gille et al., 2014; Panassa et al., 2018). However, wind stress curl and Ekman pumping behave differently
depending on location. South of 50◦S, Ekman pumping drives upwelling while north of 50◦S, Ekman pumping
can induce downwelling (Gille et al., 2014). Nevertheless, wind stress curl on the Kerguelen Plateau has been
useful for investigating marine heatwaves, an important climate extreme that may become more frequent in
the future (Oliver et al., 2018). Wind stress curl and Ekman pumping during the winter following a marine
heatwave led to downwelling, allowing warmer water to penetrate further down the water column and for
the heatwave to persist longer (Su et al., 2020). Wind stress curl was not included in our study , but may be
considered in future analyses with the model.
Local environmental conditions are closely linked to large-scale climate drivers (Jones et al., 2016). In our
study , cold SST and periods of higher than average wind speed were related to negative SAM phases. This
contrasts with other climate studies correlating negative SST anomalies with positive SAM phases across the
Southern Ocean (Lovenduski and Gruber, 2005). As indicated above, the oceanography of the Kerguelen
Plateau is complex and spatially heterogeneous. Lovenduski and Gruber (2005) found that positive SAM
phases in the southern Indian Ocean lead to cold upwelling in the Antarctic Zone (south of the Polar Front)
and downwelling in the sub-Antarctic zone (40-50◦S). Other spatial investigations of these trends found that
ocean temperature on the Kerguelen Plateau signiﬁcantly correlated with the El Niño Southern Oscillation
in the north and the SAM index over the whole plateau (Su et al., 2020). Future analyses may need to
incorporate spatial variation in order to identify the areas where the food web will be most affected by these
changes.
Food web dynamics signiﬁcantly correlated with summer environmental trends and this is consistent with
other studies showing strong summer environmental trends (Lovenduski and Gruber, 2005; Panassa et al.,
2018; Meredith et al., 2019; Fonseca and Martín-Torres, 2019). Stronger correlations during summer could
be a result of the trends used to calibrate the model. Some of the observed annual trends were derived from
animal counts during the breeding season in summer (e.g. Weimerskirch et al. 2018). However, other trends
used in this study were not derived from counts during the breeding season (e.g. the CPUE data) and outputs
from the preferred model reproduced annual trends from other studies (Gasco et al. 2019, see Appendix B).
69
Chapter 4
Therefore, if there was any bias toward summer from the trend data, it may not be strong. Summer is an
important season for the Kerguelen Plateau food web; seabirds and seals breed and forage during this time of
year (Delord et al., 2014). A positive summer SAM trend has occurred over the last decade (Meredith et al.,
2019; Panassa et al., 2018) and our model output suggests that continued positive SAM events could reduce
the productivity of the food web, especially with regards to the Patagonian toothﬁsh.
4.4.3 Environmental impacts on the Patagonian toothﬁsh
Peaks in juvenile biomass correlated positively with annual and summer zonal wind. Peaks in adult biomass
correlated positively with winter zonal wind anomalies and positively with summer SAM. Mixing induced
by zonal wind could increase prey availability for toothﬁsh, leading to increased biomass. The positive cor-
relation seen with summer SAM could be indicative of downwelling on the Kerguelen Plateau, increasing
prey availability for adult Patagonian toothﬁsh located deeper into the water column. This result could be
related to the catchability of toothﬁsh rather than biomass itself as CPUE was used as a proxy for biomass.
Increased prey availability can affect ﬁsh behaviour and make toothﬁsh more vulnerable to being caught
(Arreguín-Sánchez, 1996; Parkes et al., 1996).
When we investigated time-lagged correlations, we found stronger correlations when we introduced a three
and four season lag (equivalent to 1.5 and 2 year lag, respectively). Lagged juvenile biomass correlated nega-
tively with summer SST and summer and winter SAM anomalies. Lagged adult biomass correlated negatively
with summer SST and winter SAM at the four season lag.
Our model output produced the strongest correlation between juvenile toothﬁsh and summer SST at the four
season lag (2 year lag). This is consistent with results reported by Belchier and Collins (2008) who identiﬁed
summer SST impacts on juveniles. Juvenile toothﬁsh abundance (older than one year) in South Georgia was
inversely correlated with summer SST experienced by adults prior to spawning. Belchier and Collins (2008)
suggested that prey availability linked to large-scale climatic processes could be driving this variability . This
is consistent with our study as lagged juvenile biomass correlated with the SAM, which has been identiﬁed as
the underlying driver of prey biomass in our model.
There is no clear reason for the lagged correlations seen between adult toothﬁsh biomass, summer SST and
winter SAM. This could be related to catchability in the model. However, studies have reported toothﬁsh
showing strong year classes every 4–5 years (Laptikhovsky and Brickle, 2005); perhaps further separation
of toothﬁsh into speciﬁc age classes may clarify these correlations. Untangling these relationships will help
provide reliable estimates of life history traits that are required for effective management of the ﬁshery (Yates
et al., 2018).
4.5 Conclusion
Our study found that ﬁshing was not a driver of food web dynamics and supports other recent ﬁndings of
sustainable current ﬁshing of Patagonian toothﬁsh on the Kerguelen Plateau, with respect to effects on both
toothﬁsh biomass and food web dynamics. Our ﬁndings also highlight the importance of bottom-up processes
driving the food web for the last 40 years. Cold and well-mixed waters lead to productive food web dynamics
on the Kerguelen Plateau and these conditions appear to be linked to negative SAM phases, based on our
model results. A continued positive SAM trend, as has been observed over the last decade, could lead to
reduced prey availability and reduced predator populations. However, due to the spatial heterogeneity in
70
Chapter 4
oceanographic processes on the Kerguelen Plateau, more work is needed to clarify these effects. Our results
also suggest that Patagonian toothﬁsh recruitment is inﬂuenced by the environment, which is an important
consideration for ﬁsheries management, particularly if patterns of variability change under future climate
change.
71
CHAPTER 5
Spatially explicit food web modelling to consider
ﬁshing impacts and marine protected areas on
the Kerguelen Plateau
Abstract
Spatial management strongly supports approaches for the conservation of Southern Ocean ecosystems. How-
ever, to date, there are only a very limited number of spatially explicit modelling tools to inform ecosystem-
based management for the Southern Ocean. The Kerguelen Plateau, in the Indian Sector of the Southern
Ocean, is an ecological hotspot and an important region for the Patagonian toothﬁsh ﬁshery . Fishing areas
overlap with foraging regions for predators, which has had, and may continue to have, impacts on predator
species. Two marine protected areas (MPAs) have been implemented on the Kerguelen Plateau to protect
important pelagic and benthic habitats for species in the region. In this study , we developed two spatially-
explicit food web models, one that includes ﬁshing activity and one with no ﬁshing activity present to consider
spatial ﬁshing impacts and food web representation in MPAs. Particular attention was paid to developing a
robust model that sufﬁciently replicated spatial food web dynamics. Comparisons between the non-ﬁshed and
ﬁshed model showed decreases in Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass associated with ﬁshing activity , which was
consistent with other studies. A higher decrease was found for the northern region of the Kerguelen Plateau
compared to the southern region, most likely due to higher catch limits and longer ﬁshing seasons in the
northern region. However, overall we found limited direct impacts of Patagonian toothﬁsh ﬁshing on stocks
or on the food web over time and space. Groups in the food web were well distributed within MPAs, indicating
that these areas are representative of species’ distributions and food web processes. Primary productivity was
a large determinant of food web distribution and foraging habitat. Our models provide an important baseline
for evaluating the trophic effects of ﬁshing and climate change to inform ecosystem-based management.
5.1 Introduction
The management of Southern Ocean ecosystems is important for conserving its unique biodiversity and sup-
porting sustainable ﬁshing. The large domain and varied environments mean that it is inadequate to consider
the region (or even a sub-region) as a homogeneous entity . Therefore, understanding the spatial differences in
habitats, species assemblages and food web structure and function is necessary . This can be difﬁcult to achieve
as the spatial distributions of organisms in an ecosystem are inﬂuenced by many factors. The distributions of
phytoplankton and zooplankton are inﬂuenced by environmental conditions, including temperature, salinity ,
currents and nutrient availability , while higher predators are inﬂuenced to a greater or lesser degree by prey
aggregations (Constable et al., 2014). Prey aggregations can be used to determine important foraging regions
Chapter 5
for predators; however, they may be difﬁcult to predict as predators may still be present in foraging regions
after their prey have gone. Moreover, many ecologically important Southern Ocean regions are isolated and
therefore difﬁcult to consistently monitor.
The Kerguelen Plateau is an important, yet isolated, region for Southern Ocean ecosystems (Hindell et al.,
2020). Water passing over the plateau is enriched with iron from the continental shelf, resulting in annual
phytoplankton blooms that form the foundation of the food web (Park et al., 2014). The eastward ﬂow
of the Antarctic Circumpolar Current and associated oceanography (i.e. upwelling, downwelling and eddy
dynamics) restricts these blooms to the east of the plateau and downstream (Hindell et al., 2011; Koubbi
et al., 2016). Habitat modelling and tracking studies have shown that many predators forage in the areas
coinciding with these blooms (Thiers et al., 2017; Hindell et al., 2011; Delord et al., 2014).
In addition to its ecological importance, the Kerguelen Plateau is also a site for an economically important
commercial ﬁshery . The plateau consists of both French and Australian Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZ, Fig
5.1). Operating within each EEZ, is a ﬁshery that targets Patagonian toothﬁsh. Patagonian toothﬁsh are a
large demersal ﬁsh (up to 2 m in length) with a long life cycle. Juvenile toothﬁsh inhabit pelagic waters
and start to settle in benthic habitat after 13–15 months of age (Péron et al., 2016) while adults inhabit
depths up to 2500 m (Mintenbeck, 2017). Fishing occurs in areas that overlap with the foraging activity of
higher predators. As a result, observations have shown associated negative impacts on predators from ﬁshing
activity . For example, incidental seabird mortality may have reduced populations of frequently killed species
(white-chinned petrels and grey petrels, Delord et al. 2010), which presents a potentially serious threat for
these species on the Kerguelen Plateau (Barbraud et al., 2009).
The Commission for the Conservation of Antarctic Marine Living Resources (CCAMLR) uses an ecosystem-
based approach to managing ﬁsheries. CCAMLR has implemented conservation measures for the Patago-
nian toothﬁsh ﬁshery to reduce ecological impacts such as changing the ﬁshing season, night-setting, using
weighted lines and bird scaring devices (Duhamel et al., 2019). These strategies have signiﬁcantly reduced in-
cidental seabird mortality (CCAMLR, 2018a). CCAMLR and its member states have also adopted spatial tools,
such as implementing marine protected areas (MPAs), to conserve and manage human impacts on Southern
Ocean ecosystems (Brooks et al., 2016).
Two MPAs have been implemented on the Kerguelen Plateau by France and Australia, one each in the French
and Australian EEZ (Fig 5.1). Priority zones of ecological interest were identiﬁed during an ecoregionalisation
study for the French EEZ, which led to the implementation of a nature reserve in 2006 that was extended
in 2016 (Azam et al., 2019). To conserve the unique ecology and biodiversity in the Australian EEZ, a fully
no-take MPA was created in 2002. The main ecological objectives of the MPA are to protect the biodiversity
and unique features of the benthic and pelagic environments. This includes representative portions of the
different marine habitat types (i.e. foraging regions used by land-based predators such as penguins) and
important benthic habitat such as those of taxa vulnerable to demersal ﬁshing (Brooks et al., 2019; Welsford
et al., 2014).
Habitat modelling and ecoregionalisation studies have focused on either predators, speciﬁc species or groups
of species to map important habitat for the Kerguelen Plateau food web. They have also been used to consider
the level of ecosystem protection provided by MPAs (e.g. Brooks et al. 2020; Martin et al. 2019). Spatially-
resolved food web models build upon these insights to explore current ecosystem-based management strate-
gies at the food web level, identify important areas for ecosystems and consider the spatially-explicit ecosystem
impact of ﬁshing (e.g Dahood 2017). In this study , we developed a spatially-resolved ecosystem model for the
73
Chapter 5
Kerguelen Plateau using Ecospace. Ecospace is a software for developing spatially-explicit food web models
developed in Ecopath with Ecosim.
The previous chapter identiﬁed temporal drivers of ecosystem change on the Kerguelen Plateau over a 32-year
period (Chapter 4, Subramaniam et al. 2020c). This study identiﬁed bottom-up drivers of food web changes,
especially for Patagonian toothﬁsh. Here, we extend the model from Chapter 4 to explore the spatial impacts
of ﬁshing on the food web as well as to compare food web structure inside and outside MPAs on the plateau.
An emphasis on this study was establishing a robust model that would sufﬁciently reproduce spatial food web
dynamics and species’ distributions in the model domain.

5.2 Methods
5.2.1 Ecopath with Ecosim
Ecospace builds upon models developed using Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) to run spatial simulations (Chris-
tensen et al., 2008). We used the EwE model developed for the Kerguelen Plateau (Chapters 3 and 4) as our
starting point and tuned the Ecospace models for the same period. This model comprises 28 functional groups

that were organised based on similar diets and life cycles. This is visualised in Fig 5.2 using the ’ggraph’ pack-
age in R (R Core Team, 2020) where functional groups are plotted against calculated trophic levels from the
Ecopath model in Chapter 3. Each group is linked to their prey using the diet matrix and weighted by diet
fraction.

Figure 5.2. Food web modiﬁed from (Subramaniam et al., 2020c) presents energy ﬂow through each trophic
level on the Kerguelen Plateau. Each node (circle) represents functional groups listed on the right, linkages
between groups represent diet of each group (coloured from predator to prey) and width of linkage indicates
proportion of diet.
The balanced model was calibrated with biomass and ﬁshing time-series data from a 32-year time series
(1986–2018), using a step-wise ﬁtting approach (Chapter 4, Subramaniam et al. 2020c). We developed two
Ecospace models: one that includes ﬁshing activity and one with no ﬁshing activity present. We applied
the model presented in Subramaniam et al. (2020c) that did not include ﬁshing activity as a driver of food
web dynamics for the Ecospace model without ﬁshing activity . We used the best-ﬁtting Ecosim model that
included ﬁshing activity as a driver of food web dynamics to develop the corresponding Ecospace model. The
only difference between these models was the vulnerability parameters that described trophic interactions in
the Ecosim model. A (unit-less) value in the range of 1–100 is assigned to each trophic interaction to dictate
how consumption is inﬂuenced by changes in predator and prey biomass. Values close to 1 indicate bottom-
up control while values close to 100 indicate that top-down inﬂuences dominate. In the Ecosim model with
ﬁshing activity , top-down (i.e. predation) inﬂuences were more important compared to the model with no
ﬁshing activity (Table 5.1 and 5.2 respectively). Additionally , more vulnerability parameters were adjusted
to ﬁt the Ecosim model with ﬁshing activity .

5.2.2 Ecospace
Ecospace resolves the time-dynamic (spatially aggregated) Ecosim model using a two-dimensional grid. Spa-
tial simulations are initiated by distributing the aggregated biomass from the underlying Ecopath model homo-
geneously across all grid cells (Christensen et al., 2014) before allowing that biomass to redistribute according
to prescribed rules.
The immigration rate consists of four emigration ﬂows from the surrounding cells. The emigration ﬂows
(Bout,r,c,i) are represented as instantaneous movement rates mi times the biomass density in the cell (Br,c,i):
Bout,r,c,i =
4∑
d=1
mi,d× Br,c,i
where (r, c) is cell row and column and d is movement direction (up, down, left, or right). Instantaneous
emigration rates mi from a given cell in Ecospace are assumed to vary based on the functional group, habitat
preferences and responses of organisms to predation risk and feeding conditions (Christensen et al., 2008).
5.2.3 Ecospace model parameterisation
Ecospace uses characteristics such as habitat type and depth to create maps that assign functional groups to
cells. We chose a grid resolution of 0.25◦ x 0.25◦ to match with available observations of animal tracking data
(e.g. Thiers et al. 2017). We applied this resolution to the model domain (shown in Fig 5.1) to produce a 44 x
80 grid. Our underlying base map used to specify grid cells was comprised of a bathymetry layer downloaded
from the General Bathymetric Chart of the Oceans (https://www.gebco.net/) and resampled to 0.25◦ x
0.25◦ in ArcGIS.
Additional map layers were included as presence-absence (grid cells assigned as 1 or 0, respectively). The
French and Australian EEZs were input as separate layers and split into regions where the ﬁsheries operate
and do not operate, including a layer for no-take MPAs. Depth layers were split into 0–199, 200–499, 500–
999, 1000–1999, 2000+ to specify foraging depths and habitats for speciﬁc functional groups. These were
originally shapeﬁles that were converted into grids using the ’fasterize’ package in R and the bathymetry layer
as a reference (Ross, 2018). A map layer was created for macroalgae as occurring within the top 100 m close
to the islands (Koubbi et al., 2016).
The dispersal rate ( mi) describes the random movement of a functional group from a given position and is
expressed as distance travelled per year (km y−1). We used dispersal rates from the Ecospace model developed
for the West Antarctic Peninsula (Dahood, 2017) for all functional groups except for Patagonian toothﬁsh
(Table 5.3).
Patagonian toothﬁsh are a slow growing, long lived ﬁsh species. Juveniles ( < 75cm, Collins et al. 2010;
Pruvost et al. 2005) inhabit waters up to 1000 m deep and adults ( > 75 cm) are benthic dwelling, living
at depths between 600 m and 2500 m (Mintenbeck, 2017). A recent analysis of tagging data reported that
most ﬁsh were captured within 50 km of their release location, although there were outliers who travelled
distances more than 500 km (Burch et al., 2019). To match modelled adult spatial distribution to observations,
we tested dispersal parameters of 25, 50, 150 and 300 km y−1, along with foraging usage and habitat capacity
maps (described below). Tagging data were limited to those selected via trawl or longline gear ( > 30 cm),
therefore, the dispersal rate of juveniles less than 30 cm was unknown but likely to be higher than adults.
We chose to use a dispersal rate of 300 km y−1 as this equated to less than 1 km d−1. We consider this value
77
Chapter 5
reasonable given the likely movement of Patagonian toothﬁsh towards deeper benthic habitats as they age
past one year (Péron et al., 2016).
The dispersal rates of functional groups are assumed to be higher in non-preferred habitats, implying that
they will try to leave these habitats to seek out preferred habitat (Christensen et al., 2008). This is referred to
in Ecospace as ’relative dispersal in bad habitats’ and is expressed as a multiplier of mi between 2–10 with a
default value of 5. We kept the default values for this study . The increased vulnerability of functional groups
to predation outside of their preferred habitat is parameterised as a multiplier between 2–10, with a default
value of 2 (a value of 1 corresponds to no change in dispersal). We chose to remain with the default values in
our study as there were no functional groups that would have been exceptionally vulnerable or invulnerable
in non-preferred habitats (Table 5.3).

Functional groups were assigned to habitat layers by allocating biomass proportions using the habitat foraging
usage parameter. Macroalgae biomass was restricted to the macroalgae layer. Sperm whales prefer to forage
in waters deeper than 1000 m and feed on demersal prey; therefore, their biomass was restricted to these
layers (Dahood, 2017). We allowed juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh to disperse evenly across their habitat
(habitat map described below). However, as adult Patagonian toothﬁsh are found at depths > 600 m, we
allocated their biomass to deeper waters (Péron et al., 2016). Small mesopelagic ﬁsh distribution was forced
so that more ﬁsh occurred close to the islands. Large mesopelagic ﬁsh and large deep-sea demersal ﬁsh
were parameterised using distribution maps from trawl surveys (Duhamel et al., 2019). Studies on benthic
ecoregions or habitats suggest that benthic habitat extends from the islands to around 1000 m deep (e.g.
Douglass et al. 2014; Martin et al. 2019). Based on this, we conﬁned zoobenthic biomass to regions with a
depth less than 1000 m, with more biomass allocated between 500–1000 m. Foraging usage parameters are
presented in Table 5.4.

Maps from a recent habitat modelling study in the Southern Ocean were used as priors to inform the spatial
distribution of functional groups in Ecospace (Hindell et al., 2020). Circumpolar maps of habitat importance
79
Chapter 5
produced from the study were cropped to our model domain and resampled to 0.25◦ x 0.25◦. These were input
as habitat capacity maps, expressed as proportions of 0–1, with 1 being most preferred habitat (Christensen
et al., 2014; Coll et al., 2019).
King penguin, macaroni penguin and royal penguin habitat importance were averaged to create map input
for penguins. Black-browed albatrosses, wandering albatrosses, grey-headed albatrosses and light-mantled
sooty albatross maps were averaged to give a combined habitat capacity map for albatrosses. White-chinned
petrel habitat importance was used to deﬁne habitat capacity for other seabirds and humpback whale habitat
importance was used for baleen whales. Habitat importance maps for Antarctic fur seals were re-scaled for a
clearer understanding of the important areas for this species on the Kerguelen Plateau (see Appendix).
Additional maps were created for sperm whales and Patagonian toothﬁsh to further tune the model. Sperm
whales have not been observed within the Australian EEZ, therefore we restricted their distribution to the
French EEZ (Fig C.3a). Observations and modelling studies of Patagonian toothﬁsh distribution have been
conﬁned to the plateau (less than 2000 m) region (e.g. Péron et al. 2016; Duhamel et al. 2019). A prediction
map of female Patagonian toothﬁsh on the Kerguelen Plateau showed that ﬁsh up to 80 cm length are dis-
tributed at depths up to 1000 m, while bigger ﬁsh are likely to occur at depths greater than 1000 m. Based
on this study , we created maps to conﬁne juveniles within the 1000 m contour (Fig C.3b) and adults within
the 2000 m contour (Fig C.3c).
Spatial variation in primary productivity on the Kerguelen Plateau was modelled by incorporating a pri-
mary productivity map. This map is expressed as a multiplier value that is applied to the baseline produc-
tion/biomass (P /B) for phytoplankton in the underlying Ecopath model (Christensen et al., 2008). Note
that this layer is static and remains constant throughout the Ecospace simulation. We calculated the primary
productivity multiplier using the following equation and a ten year average (2009-2019) of remotely sensed
chlorophyll a (MODISA) at our grid resolution (Johnson et al., 2017):
Mi = Chla i
Chla ave
where M is the multiplier value for each grid cell i, Chl i is Chl a concentration in i and Chl ave is average Chl
a concentration for the whole region (Fig 5.3).
Fishing activity was driven through Ecosim using ﬁshing effort (F E) calculated as:
F E = C
C PU E
where F E is number of hooks, C is annual ﬁsheries catch in kg and C PU E is annual catch per unit effort as
kg per hook for longline. For the trawl gear, F E was km2, C was in tonnes and C PU E was tonnes per km2.
Effort was calculated for each year and divided by the number of months for each respective ﬁshing season
to give monthly effort (10 months for French longline, 7 months for Australian longline and 12 months for
Australian trawl). Fishing on the Kerguelen Plateau occurs within the EEZs and longline ﬁshing is restricted
to depths of 500–2000 m. The French longline ﬁshery was allocated to the French EEZ in the 500–2000 m
layer, Australian longline was allocated to the Australian EEZ 500–2000 m layer and Australian trawl was
allocated to the entire Australian EEZ. All ﬁshing activity was restricted from occurring in no-take MPA layers
(Fig C.4). A simple gravity model spreads annual ﬁshing effort values from Ecosim across all grid cells open
to ﬁshing proportionally to the relative proﬁtability of ﬁshing in that cell (see Christensen et al. 2008 for more
detail).
