Chapter 5
Figure 5.3. Primary productivity multiplier map input for Ecospace.
5.2.4 Model evaluation
While it is not yet possible to statistically ﬁt the model, it can be tuned to replicate observed spatial distribution
patterns and compared to these observations (Romagnoni et al., 2015). Model performance was evaluated by
performing Spearman rank correlations between simulations from both the non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed Ecospace
models and the inputs maps. We only correlated the biomass distribution of groups for which a habitat
capacity map was used to parameterise distribution. These groups were baleen whales, Antarctic fur seals,
southern elephant seals, penguins, albatrosses, other seabirds. A difference map was created for groups with
differing distributions between non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed scenarios. Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass distribution
was visually compared to trawl survey data (Duhamel et al., 2019).
5.2.5 Exploring spatial impacts
Spatial impacts of ﬁshing were evaluated by calculating the percentage change in Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass
between the non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed model. Changes in food web dynamics between the models were consid-
ered by performing an inverse multidimensional scaling ordination (MDS) (Groenen and Borg, 2014). Grid-
ded model output was organised into a matrix with each row representing a single grid cell and columns
corresponding to biomass for each functional group. Spearman rank correlations were used to create a cor-
relation matrix that was used then used to calculate a measure of dissimilarity (calculated as 1-correlation).
The inverse mode MDS was performed with the dissimilarity matrix by treating biomass for each functional
group as a sample. Functional groups were clustered using the K-means clustering algorithm (Hartigan and
Wong, 1979). Clusters in the visualised MDS show functional groups that were positively correlated.
The representativeness of MPAs in the model was considered by calculating the proportion of modelled
biomass (over the whole domain) that is distributed within MPAs using the ’exactextractr’ package in R (Bas-
81
Chapter 5
ton, 2020). The whole French EEZ is regarded as an MPA and within this MPA are sections with varying
degrees of protection (Azam et al., 2019). The MPA consists of protection zones surrounding the nature
reserve (green sections in Fig 5.4), a strict protection zone (navy section in Fig 5.4) and no-take regions
(blue sections in Fig 5.4). The Australian EEZ MPA consists of a no-take area (orange region in Fig 5.4). We
calculated modelled biomass distributed in each of these sections.
Figure 5.4. Marine protected areas on the Kerguelen Plateau.

5.3 Results
5.3.1 Model evaluation
Model representation of adult Patagonian toothﬁsh distribution was considered most realistic when their
dispersal rate was set to 150 km y−1. At this rate of movement, more adults were distributed towards deeper
waters (dark coloured sections in Fig 5.5). Resulting spatial distributions from each tested dispersal rate are
presented in Fig C.2. Modelled Patagonian toothﬁsh distribution was compared with periodic trawl surveys
conducted in the French EEZ (Duhamel et al., 2019). Spatial distributions from the most recent survey
conducted in 2017 were compared with our results (Fig 5.6a). As gridded output from the model represents
an average estimated biomass over a region while the POKER data are provided for single points (i.e., at a
ﬁner scale), it is unlikely that the point data is representative of the gridded data. Therefore, our comparison
was restricted to the spatial distribution of biomass. Modelled biomass distribution from 2017 was consistent
with observations from the trawl survey with higher biomass concentrated along the 1000 m contour and
Skiff Bank (64–66◦E, 50◦S) in both the non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed scenario (Fig 5.6b and c).
Figure 5.5. Ratio of adult biomass between 25 km y −1 and 150 km y−1 dispersal rate (baseline). Higher
values indicate movement of biomass towards deeper depths at 150 km y−1. Contours at 1000, 2000, 3000,
4000

(Table C.1). Southern elephant seal distributions for non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed scenarios were correlated with
observations (r = 0.81, p = <2.2e-16 and r = 0.84, p = <2.2e-16 respectively). Output for non-ﬁshed and
ﬁshed scenarios were correlated with each other (r = 0.99, p = <2.2e-16), although there were slight differ-

0.51, p = <2.2e-16 and ﬁshed;r = 0.54, p = <2.2e-16). Other seabirds were concentrated around Kerguelen
Island and on the east side of the Kerguelen Plateau in both scenarios (Fig 5.9h and i) and correlated better
with observations compared to albatrosses (non-ﬁshed; r = 0.67, p = < 2.2e-16 and ﬁshed; r = 0.64, p =
<2.2e-16). Baleen whale distribution was very similar to the habitat importance map input in both scenarios.
Whale biomass was slightly more concentrated on the eastern side of the Kerguelen Plateau outside of the
EEZs (Fig C.5a–c).

5.3.2 Spatial ﬁshing impacts
There was a general decrease seen in both juvenile and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass between non-
ﬁshed and ﬁshed scenarios, although there were small regions where an increase was observed. The decrease
in biomass in the ﬁshed scenario was greater in the French EEZ (Fig 5.10) than in the Australian zone. Adult
biomass decreased by 17% between the 1000 and 2000 m contours within the French EEZ, however close to
the Kerguelen Islands biomass increased up to 3% (Fig 5.10a). Decreases in biomass in the Australian EEZ
were less than 7% for both juveniles and adults. Overall, juvenile biomass decreased by 12% towards the
outer region of their distribution and increased by 8% in the north-east region of the Australian zone and
within the eastern section of the Heard Island MPA (Fig 5.10b).

Figure 5.11. Multi-dimensional scaling ordination of the modelled food web for (a) non-ﬁshed and (b) ﬁshed
scenarios. Clusters show positively correlated functional groups, with group numbers corresponding to the

Clustering in the MDS corresponded with differences in the spatial distribution of myctophids and small
mesopelagic ﬁsh between the two scenarios. This may signify two energy pathways in the food web induced
by the inclusion of ﬁshing activity in the model. We used difference maps to explore the change in spa-
tial distribution of myctophids and small mesopelagic ﬁsh between the two scenarios and present possible
representations of these energy pathways to predators based on the clustering from the MDS (Fig 5.13).
In the ﬁshed scenario, myctophid biomass increased between the Kerguelen Islands and Heard and McDonald
Island and decreased on the eastern side of the Kerguelen Islands (Fig 5.12a), coinciding with concentrated
sections of predator distributions (Fig 5.8). The corresponding energy pathway indicates that energy from
phytoplankton is transferred to myctophids in these areas of the Kerguelen Plateau in the model (Fig 5.13c). In
contrast, small mesopelagic ﬁsh decreased where myctophids increased and decreased where they increased
88
Chapter 5
(Fig 5.12b). In areas where small mesopelagic ﬁsh biomass was concentrated, energy from phytoplankton
was transferred to predators via small mesopelagic ﬁsh (Fig 5.13d). Energy pathways are contrasted in the
non-ﬁshed scenario (Fig 5.13a and b).

5.3.3 Marine protected areas
We considered the proportion of biomass over the whole domain (60-80◦E, 45-56◦S) that occurred in MPAs in
the scenario with ﬁshing activity (Fig 5.14). Patagonian toothﬁsh (groups 3 and 4) and benthic organisms had
the largest percentage distribution in both MPAs. Around 60% of Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass was distributed
within the MPA for the French EEZ, with the bulk contained in the strict protection zone (navy section, Fig
5.14c) and up to 15% in no-take areas (Fig 5.14a and green section Fig 5.14c). Ten percent of juvenile and
7% of adult Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass was distributed within the no-take area of the Australian EEZ (Fig
5.14b). Similarly , 60% of zoobenthic biomass distribution (group 8, Fig 5.14d) was covered by the Kerguelen

Chapter 5
5.4 Discussion
5.4.1 Modelled biomass distributions
Modelled Patagonian toothﬁsh distribution was consistent with observations, while predators showed prefer-
ence for the eastern side of the Kerguelen Plateau in both non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed models. This is consistent with
observed primary productivity being highest on the eastern side and downstream of the Kerguelen Plateau.
Modelled distributions correlated with input maps and were consistent with results from other tracking stud-
ies of animals tagged on the Kerguelen Plateau. In this section, we focus on the predators that live and breed
on the Kerguelen Plateau. See Appendix C for discussion of baleen whale distribution.
Tracks of Antarctic fur seals showed they spent the most time on the east and south-east of the Kerguelen
Islands, within the 1000 m contour (Thiers et al., 2017). This was consistent with our modelled spatial
distribution. Antarctic fur seal biomass was also concentrated on the eastern side of Heard and McDonald
Islands extending to the boundary of the Australian EEZ, consistent with Heard Island foraging distribution
studies (Casper et al., 2010; Goldsworthy et al., 2010).
Southern elephant seal biomass distribution in our model was concentrated on the eastern and south-eastern
side of the Kerguelen Plateau, including oceanic waters to the east (beyond the 3000 m contour). This result
was in agreement with tracking studies showing that seals travelled south-east and in oceanic waters (Thiers
et al., 2017; Hindell et al., 2011). A circumpolar seal tracking study showed that while seals did travel east
and south-east as simulated in our study , they also travelled and spent time on the western side of the plateau
(Hindell et al., 2016) and this was not captured in our simulations. Southern elephant seal distribution
is likely inﬂuenced by oceanographic features such as mesoscale eddies that aggregate prey (Bailleul et al.,
2010; Dragon et al., 2010; Green et al., 2020), which was not represented in our model. Additionally , southern
elephant seals in the north-west could be visitors from sub-Antarctic islands adjacent to the Kerguelen Plateau
(Hindell et al., 2016).
King penguins and macaroni penguins spent most of their time south-east of the Kerguelen Plateau, which
is consistent with our model results. Although they also spent time off the plateau to forage in oceanic
waters (Thiers et al., 2017; Hindell et al., 2011). This was captured in our model as penguin biomass was
concentrated between 75–80°E around 50°S in water deeper than 2000 m. Our modelled distribution is also
in agreement with a tracking study showing that macaroni penguins breeding on Heard Island remained
within the 1000 m contour east of Heard Island (Hindell et al., 2011).
Wandering albatrosses and black-browed albatrosses tagged on Kerguelen Island spent time close to and
north-east of the Kerguelen Islands, including beyond the 1000 m contour, which is captured in our model
(Thiers et al., 2017). As the north-west region of the plateau had a higher biomass, this was consistent
with observations showing wandering albatrosses travelling from the Crozet archipelago (adjacent to the
Kerguelen Plateau) to spend time on the north-west and south-east regions of the plateau (Thiers et al.,
2017). Additionally , our ﬁndings show a concentration of albatross biomass around Heard Island, which
is consistent with tracks of black-browed albatrosses at Heard Island (Hindell et al., 2011). Other seabird
biomass was concentrated north-east of the Kerguelen Islands and on the east side of the Kerguelen Plateau
including oceanic waters beyond 3000 m. This contrasted with results from Thiers et al. (2017) showing
birds travelled south of Kerguelen Islands and spent time in the waters between Kerguelen and Heard and
McDonald Islands.
92
Chapter 5
Correlations indicate that modelled biomass distribution using food web dynamics successfully replicated
habitat usage studies and predator tracks (Thiers et al., 2017; Hindell et al., 2011). However, improvements
to distributions could be made by incorporating changes to primary productivity over time. A framework
exists for incorporating monthly maps to explore the spatio-temporal variability of environmental, biological
(i.e. chlorophyll) and ﬁshing effort in Ecospace (Steenbeek et al., 2013; Dahood, 2017). This method was
out of the scope for this study; however, future implementations of the model will beneﬁt from using this
framework.
5.4.2 Spatial ﬁshing impacts
Our model indicated a larger decrease in biomass due to ﬁshing in the French EEZ compared to the Australian
EEZ. This is likely related to differences in ﬁshing activity , as represented in the model. Fishing effort and catch
limits in the French EEZ (around 5000 tonnes) are higher than the Australian EEZ (around 3000 tonnes shared
between trawl and longline). The French ﬁshing season is also longer than the Australian season (10 months
compared to 7 months). Simulated ﬁshing impacts showed a maximum decrease in biomass of 17% for adult
and 12% for juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh. This is in agreement with a 20% decrease in unﬁshed biomass
between 2002 and 2012 and is within the goals of the management plan for the Australian EEZ (Brooks et al.
2019 and references therein). Modelled decreases were also consistent with biomass ﬂuctuations between
trawl surveys on the French EEZ. Biomass ﬂuctuated by 20% (including a signiﬁcant larger change) between
surveys indicating that the Patagonian toothﬁsh population is relatively stable (Duhamel et al., 2019).
Juvenile and adult biomass increased up to 8% and 3%, respectively , in the region north-east of Kerguelen
Island. Our results suggest this region is important for Patagonian toothﬁsh and is in agreement with an
ecoregionalisation study that identiﬁed this region as well as the area surrounding the island (no-take area) as
essential habitat for Patagonian toothﬁsh (Koubbi et al., 2016). Juveniles are found in the waters surrounding
the island and spawn on the slopes of the western and southern side of the plateau (Ziegler and Welsford,
2019).
Results from our spatial model suggests that current ﬁshing of Patagonian toothﬁsh is sustainable. While there
appears to be very little direct impact of ﬁshing on Patagonian toothﬁsh over time and space (Brooks et al.,
2019; Duhamel et al., 2019; Subramaniam et al., 2020c), there may be indirect impacts on energy pathways
in the food web.
We identiﬁed potential alternative pathways between non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed models associated with changes
to the spatial distribution of organisms in the food web. These changes were related to the different vulnera-
bility parameters used to ﬁt the non-ﬁshed and ﬁshed Ecosim models to available data. In the ﬁshed Ecosim
model, many values reﬂected top-down controls of predation. Juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh speciﬁcally had
a strong top-down impact on small mesopelagic ﬁsh, but this effect was spatially variable. The top-down
effect was most evident in the region between Kerguelen Island and Heard and McDonald Island (middle
of the plateau, between 50◦S and 54◦S (Fig 5.13b). The strong top-down impact displayed in our model is
consistent with ﬁndings from other regions of the Southern Ocean. Juvenile Patagonian toothﬁsh in South
Georgia predominantly feed on yellow-ﬁnned notothen, a small ﬁsh (< 50 cm) that occurs within the top 200
m of the water column (Froese and Pauly, 2019; Collins et al., 2007). The top-down effect and alternative
pathways apparent in our model have a negligible indirect impact on the food web as no major changes in
the biomass of functional groups accompanied this change.
93
Chapter 5
5.4.3 Food web representation in MPAs
Patagonian toothﬁsh and zoobenthos were well distributed within MPAs. We found a total of 74% of zooben-
thos distribution within the MPAs on the Kerguelen Plateau (60% of zoobenthos distribution covered by the
French MPA and 14% by the Australian MPA). The large amount of benthic cover is unsurprising as protecting
vulnerable benthic marine ecosystems is a primary objective for MPAs on the Kerguelen Plateau. Our results
agree with ﬁndings from an ecoregionalisation study by Martin et al. (2019) showing that up to 80% of shal-
low ecotype distribution ( < 250 m) was covered in no-take areas on the French EEZ and up to 30% of all
ecotypes were covered within the combined strict protection zone and no-take area. Our results also agree
with ﬁndings from Brooks et al. (2020) who also calculated up to 79% of benthic ecoregions were covered by
MPAs on the Kerguelen Plateau, with 23% occurring in no-take regions. This level of protection is expected
to remain if ecoregions shift south in response to future climate change (RCP 4.5 and 8.5) although the region
may undergo high rates of extinction (Fabri-Ruiz et al., 2019; Guillaumot et al., 2018).
Up to 60% of juvenile and adult Patagonian toothﬁsh distribution occurred in the French EEZ MPA, 8% of
juveniles and 16% of adult biomass occurred in the no-take area. As our results closely matched observations
from trawl surveys in the French EEZ, this could indicate that a high percentage of toothﬁsh distribution is
protected. Seven percent and 10% of juvenile and adult distribution occurred in the Australian no-take MPA. A
modelling study of ﬁsh distribution showed over 25% of the predicted area for all assemblages occurred within
the Australian MPA (Hill et al., 2017). Patagonian toothﬁsh had a high probability of occurring within each
predicted area, which could indicate that toothﬁsh are also well-protected on the Australian EEZ. Although
we note that the predicted area for deep-sea assemblages was less represented (12% of area occurring in the
Australian MPA), which is important habitat for adults.
Up to 20% of predator and mid-trophic level groups in the food web were represented in no-take areas (16%
of modelled seal biomass and 12% of modelled seabird biomass, 14% of cephalopod, 20% of ﬁsh and 15%
of zooplankton). Evidence suggests that well placed MPAs beneﬁt predator foraging (Kirkman et al., 2016;
Klein and Watters, 2020) and that no-take MPAs are most effective for supporting recovery of ecosystems
(Sala and Giakoumi, 2017). In our model, concentrated sections of biomass distribution occurred within
no-take MPAs. This could indicate a high level of protection, although it should be noted that other studies
suggest at least 30% representation may be required for effective recovery and preservation of ecosystem
complexity , especially under climate change (Brooks et al. 2020 and references therein). Future work with
the model could use spatio-temporal indicators to compare ecosystem health inside and outside of MPAs (Coll
and Steenbeek, 2017).
5.5 Conclusion
Our study presents a robust spatial food web model that successfully reproduces spatial food web dynamics
and species’ distributions on the Kerguelen Plateau. Our model, and model output, provide an important
baseline for evaluating spatial ﬁshing and climate impacts on the Kerguelen Plateau ecosystem. Our ﬁndings
showed decreases in Patagonian toothﬁsh biomass with the inclusion of ﬁshing activity at levels similar to other
studies, suggesting stable populations and sustainable ﬁshing activity . Comparison of models with and without
ﬁshing activity suggests that, with ﬁshing, alternative energy pathways emerge in the food web. However,
this appears to be a negligible impact at the ecosystem level. Current ecosystem-based strategies appear to be
effective for maintaining Patagonian toothﬁsh populations and sustainable ﬁshing activity however, climate
94
Chapter 5
change impacts may place additional stresses on the food web. Our study shows that primary productivity was
a large determinant of foraging habitat for many groups and any changes to primary productivity will likely
ﬂow through the food web. Future work with this model could be targeted to evaluate impacts of changes
in phytoplankton on food web distribution to develop ecosystem-based management strategies that consider
climate change.
95
CHAPTER 6
Synthesis: key ﬁndings, implications and future
research

Chapter 6
The spatial heterogeneity in physical processes across the Southern Ocean inﬂuences regional differences in
food web dynamics. This variation in food web structure and function is accompanied by ﬁshing pressure
on local populations and regional-scale climate impacts. Understanding combined climate and ﬁshing im-
pacts on regional food webs is crucial for the conservation and management of Southern Ocean ecosystems.
However, due to uneven sampling efforts across the various sectors of the Southern Ocean, the Indian sector
is less studied and many gaps exist in our understanding of ecosystem dynamics. The Kerguelen Plateau
has remained relatively under-studied - especially from a food web modelling perspective - despite being a
hotspot for ecological activity in the Southern Ocean. This thesis sought to partially correct this situation
by addressing knowledge gaps in food web structure and function and the combined effects of ﬁshing and
climate for the Kerguelen Plateau ecosystem.
Speciﬁcally , this thesis sought to identify and consider:
• Major energy pathways and key groups for energy transfer in the ecosystem;
• The role of species targeted by the ﬁshery within the food web;
• Spatio-temporal impacts of ﬁshing;
• Predation as a spatio-temporal driver;
• Environmental drivers of spatio-temporal variability; and
• The effectiveness of ecosystem-based management strategies from a food web perspective.
These gaps are represented by icons in Chapter 1 (Table 1.2).
The major ﬁndings from this thesis are presented in Table 6.1 in relation to the knowledge gaps addressed in
Chapter 1. The table recalls the icons representing each knowledge gap, the key ﬁndings from each chapter
that address these gaps and future research directions to improve and explore current understanding.
This thesis is a major contribution to food web, ecosystem and ﬁsheries assessment. It provides a valuable
tool for considering climate change impacts on not only the Kerguelen Plateau ecosystem, but also Southern
Ocean ecosystems more generally . Limited data availability has been a consistent theme in this work. Firmer
conclusions could have been made with the models developed in this study had monitoring been more con-
sistent. More monitoring needs to be undertaken with goals that include providing data applicable for the
development of ecosystem assessment and food web models (Constable et al., 2016).
The work in this thesis adopted a strategic approach using the available data to build ecosystem models,
demonstrating how models can be developed and validated effectively for regions of the Southern Ocean that
are considered ‘data poor’. Throughout this study , a sufﬁcient fraction of the available data was withheld
from the model development phase to evaluate the model behaviour. Each chapter includes signiﬁcant con-
sideration and demonstration of realistic behaviours and testable predictions from the models. The results of
this evaluation process give conﬁdence for future, additional and applied uses.
Finally , there is only one published spatially explicit food web model for the Southern Ocean (Dahood, 2017).
Therefore, this work presents one of only two spatially explicit food web models for the Southern Ocean
and the ﬁrst for the ecologically signiﬁcant Kerguelen Plateau. Insights from this work, particularly spatially-
explicit insights, demonstrate the importance of developing an understanding of spatio-temporal food web
dynamics when considering either climate change, ﬁshing impacts or both external drivers applied together.
97
Chapter 6
These insights are crucial when considering effective approaches for ecosystem-based management in the
Southern Ocean.
98
Chapter 6
Table 6.1. Major ﬁndings from this thesis in relation to the knowledge gaps presented in Chapter 1.

Appendices
APPENDIX A
Parameterisation of the Ecopath model
This appendix details the calculations, references and input parameters used in the Ecopath model for the
Kerguelen Plateau. Explicit details are provided on the decisions made for each functional group and the
range of calculations used for each parameter. Our model domain covers 60◦-80◦E and 45◦-56◦S, resulting in
an area (A) of 1,720,348 km2.
A.1 Baleen whales
The Kerguelen Plateau is a foraging region for baleen whales, however no abundance surveys have been
conducted for this region. Surveys for whale abundance have been conducted south of 60◦S since the 1990s
with most recent surveys conducted in the early 2000s (e.g Matsuoka et al. 2006; Branch 2011). As whale
populations are growing annually (e.g. Bannister et al. 2015), population estimates from these surveys have
become outdated. Additionally , whale species that occur further north (and within the model domain) such
as ﬁn and sei whales, are under-represented in these surveys. It is unclear how many whales pass through the
Kerguelen Plateau as they migrate to Antarctica, therefore we chose to extrapolate minimum and maximum
biomass estimates for this area using existing abundance surveys and exclude whales for which there were
no recent estimates.
Baleen whales that have been observed or historically caught around the Kerguelen Plateau are pygmy blue
whales, ﬁn whales, sei whales, southern right whales, humpback whales and dwarf minke whales (De Broyer
et al., 2014). Baleen whales species included in this model are ﬁn whales and southern right whales. Al-
though abundance estimates are available for sei whales, their populations are most likely underestimated
or unreliable, therefore we have excluded them from our model. Humpback whales are excluded as the only
evidence of their presence on the Kerguelen Plateau is a track of one single female humpback whale that
remained in the area for 7 weeks before travelling elsewhere (Bestley et al., 2019).
A.1.1 Biomass
For migratory animals on the Kerguelen Plateau, we followed methods described in other Ecopath models
and scaled biomass by the time spent in the area (or time spent foraging).
Biomass (in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is the population, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the
region, and A is area of model domain.
Density was converted to biomass by:
B = D× Wt× F
Appendix A
where D is density ,Wt is weight in tonnes andF is fraction of the year that the species is present in the region.
A.1.2 Other parameters
We took P/B from Hill et al. (2012) and Q /B from Hill et al. (2012) and Pinkerton et al. (2010). Q /B was
averaged to get initial Q /B for baleen whales (Table A.1). Unassimilated consumption and other mortality
was taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010). Diet composition was compiled using a combination of diets for
baleen whales presented in Hill et al. (2012), Pinkerton et al. (2010), and Pruvost et al. (2005) (Table A.2).

A.2 Killer whales
Observations of whales on the Kerguelen Plateau have recorded them in low numbers (Roche et al., 2007),
therefore we chose a low biomass for killer whales (Table A.3). Diet composition was a combination of
diet information from Pauly et al. (1998), Pinkerton et al. (2010), and Gurney et al. (2014). As they are
also natural predators of Patagonian toothﬁsh (Tixier et al., 2019), we have added this species to their diet
composition (Table A.4).
A.3 Toothed whales and dolphins
This group contains beaked whale and dolphin species foraging on the Kerguelen Plateau. Many species
of beaked whales have been observed in Antarctic waters, a detailed description of their distribution can
be found in MacLeod et al. (2005) and sightings on the Kerguelen Plateau are reported in Robineau and
Duhamel (2006) and Guiler et al. (1987). Of the species reported in sightings on the Kerguelen Plateau, the
southern bottlenose whale is reported to be the most abundant beaked whale species in the Southern Ocean
(Kasamatsu and Joyce, 1995; MacLeod et al., 2005; De Broyer et al., 2014) and have been surveyed south of
60◦S (Hakamada and Matsuoka, 2008). We used density from Hakamada and Matsuoka (2008) to estimate

Appendix A
biomass for southern bottlenose whales (Table A.5). Of the dolphins listed in Robineau and Duhamel (2006),
hourglass dolphins have been surveyed south of 60◦S. We used a density estimate from Leaper et al. (2008)
to estimate biomass (Table A.5). Beaked whales display a strong seasonal behaviour, moving towards the
north of the Antarctic continent at the end of summer (De Broyer et al., 2014). To reﬂect this seasonality , we
took the number of days spent in the Southern Ocean from Leaper et al. (2008) and divided by 365 to get
the fraction of the year they are present on the Kerguelen Plateau.
A.3.1 Biomass
Density was converted to biomass by:
B = D× Wt× F
where D is density ,Wt is weight in tonnes andF is fraction of the year that the species is present in the region.
A.3.2 Other parameters
We took P/B and from other Ecopath models and weighted them by its proportion of the total biomass (south-
ern bottlenose dolphin= 97%, hourglass dolphin= 3%) to get P/B for our model. Q/B for southern bottlenose
whales was taken from the Ecopath model for the Ross Sea while Q /B for the hourglass dolphin was calcu-
lated using percentage of body weight consumed per day . Leaper et al. (2008) report that hourglass dolphins
consume 8% of their body weight per day , this converts to a Q/B of 0.08× 365 = 29.2 y−1. Q /B was weight
by proportion biomass to get Q/B for our model. Unassimilated consumption and other mortality was taken
from Pinkerton et al. (2010) (Table A.5).
A.3.3 Diet composition
Southern bottlenose whales comprised the majority of biomass. We used diet compositions from models that
included toothed whales to form our diet composition (Hill et al., 2012; Pauly et al., 1998; Pinkerton et al.,
2010). We also assumed that due to their deep-diving behaviour (De Broyer et al., 2014), toothﬁsh were a
small component of their diet (Table A.6).
A.4 Sperm whale
Only male sperm whales are found south of 40◦S (De Broyer et al., 2014). There are no population surveys
for sperm whales north of 60 ◦S in the vicinity of the Kerguelen Plateau, however a study on sperm whale
interaction with the ﬁshery reported a number of sperm whales present on the Kerguelen Plateau. We used
this number to estimate biomass (Labadie et al., 2018) (Table A.7).
Biomass (in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is the population, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the
region, and A is area of model domain.
We used consumption and production parameters that were calculated for and used in other Ecopath models.
We averaged P/B values from Pinkerton et al. (2010), Bradford-Grieve et al. (2003), and Hill et al. (2012) for

A.5 Seals
Three species of seal (Antarctic fur seal, sub-Antarctic fur seal and southern elephant seal) occur on the Ker-
guelen Plateau. We only include Antarctic fur seals and southern elephant seals in our model as populations
of sub-Antarctic fur seals on the islands are small (8 breeding seals, Page et al. 2003) and are most likely a
hybrid species with the Antarctic fur seal (Page et al., 2003). Each species was included into the model as
an individual group due to their differing diet compositions. Antarctic fur seals consume more myctophids
than southern elephant seals (see Tables A.10 and A.12) and likely have different trophic positions in the food
web.

A.6.1 Biomass
Adult Antarctic fur seal population (N) was estimated using the 1:4.1 pup conversion in Page et al. (2003).
Biomass (B, in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is the population, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the
region, and A is area of model domain.
A.6.2 Other parameters
We used P/B from Hill et al. (2012) and calculated Q/B using the equation in Pinkerton et al. (2006). Unas-
similated consumption and other mortality were taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010). Our diet composition
was a composite of diets from Cherel et al. (2005), Lea et al. (2002) and Gurney et al. (2014) and is presented
in Table A.10.

A.7 Southern elephant seal
A.7.1 Biomass
Southern elephant seals are present on the Kerguelen Plateau all year but forage 20% of the year so we
considered forage time as the fraction of the year present (Cherel et al., 2005).
Biomass (B, in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is the population, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the
region, and A is area of model domain. Biomass is presented in Table A.11.
A.7.2 Other parameters
We took P/B from the South Georgia Ecopath model (Hill et al., 2012) and Q/B from an early version of the
Ecopath model for the Ross Sea (Pinkerton et al., 2006). Unassimilated consumption and other mortality
were taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010) (Table A.11).
A.7.3 Diet composition
Overall diet composition was taken from Cherel et al. (2005). We modiﬁed this composition for ﬁsh groups
using Slip (1995). Addtionally , we used diet composition from Pruvost et al. (2005) to modify the benthic and
Patagonian toothﬁsh component of the diet, from Gurney et al. (2014) to modify the myctophid component
and from Hill et al. (2012) to modify the mackerel iceﬁsh component (Table A.12).
A.8 Penguins
Four species of penguin live and breed on the Kerguelen Plateau: gentoo, king, macaroni and southern rock-
hopper.

Appendix A
A.8.1 Biomass
Biomass (B, in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is average number of individuals, calculated by doubling the midpoint of breeding pairs in Table
A.13, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the region and A is area
of model domain. We assumed the fraction of the year that penguins spent on Heard Island was the same as
time spent on Kerguelen Island.
A.8.2 Other parameters
P/B was taken from other models or calculated from survival rates (P/B = 1−survival rate). Average survival
rate was used for cases where multiple survival rates were found. P /B for each species was weighted by its
biomass proportion to give P /B for the whole group. Q /B was taken from other models or from studies on
seabird consumption on the Kerguelen Islands. Q/B was also weighted by biomass proportion to give Q/B for
the whole group (Table A.14). Unassimilated consumption and other mortality were taken from Pinkerton
et al. (2010).
A.8.3 Diet composition
We combined the diet of each species, focusing on those with the highest contribution to penguin biomass
(macaroni penguin) to get diet composition for the model. Diets are presented in Table A.15. Gentoo penguins
comprised a small proportion of total penguin biomass, therefore we chose not to incoporate zoobenthos into
the model diet.
A.9 Albatrosses
Six species of albatross live and breed on the Kerguelen Plateau, two of which live and breed on Heard Island.
As these birds forage over the whole plateau (De Broyer et al., 2014), we chose to assume they occur over
the entire plateau, thus did not include a habitat fraction.
A.9.1 Biomass
Biomass (in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is average number of individuals, calculated by doubling the midpoint of breeding pairs in Table
A.16, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the region, and A is area
of model domain.
A.9.2 Production and consumption
P/B was taken from other models or calculated from survival rates (P /B = 1–survival rate), which were
weighted by biomass proportion to give P/B for the whole group. Q/B was taken from other models or from

A.10 Other seabirds
Petrels, prions, fulmars, and skuas were put in this group.
A.10.1 Biomass
Biomass (in t km−2) was calculated using the following equation:
B = N× Wt× F
A
where N is average number of individuals, calculated by doubling the midpoint of breeding pairs in Table
A.19, Wt is weight in tonnes, F is fraction of the year that the species is present in the region, and A is area
of model domain.
A.10.2 Production and consumption
We used mortality rates from species that made up most of the biomass in Table A.19. These were the Antarctic
prion, common diving-petrel, South Georgia diving-petrel, and the white-chinned petrel. P/B was taken from
other models or calculated from survival rates (P/B = 1-survival rate). Q/B was taken from other models or
from studies on seabird consumption on the Kerguelen Plateau (Table A.20).
A.10.3 Diet
We combined diets from multiple species including those that feed on carrion to deﬁne diet for other seabirds;
these diet compositions are presented in Table A.21. Although mesozooplankton and zoobenthos are eaten
by some species, they were not common in the diets of the remaining seabird species, therefore we chose not
to incorporate these into the model diet.

A.11 Cephalopods
We used Ecopath to estimate biomass and updated P /B from Pruvost et al. (2005), as it seemed to low
compared to cephalopod groups in other Ecopath models. We took P/B of 6.7−1 from Hill et al. (2012) and
Pinkerton et al. (2010). We used a P/Q value of 39% from Pinkerton et al. (2010) to calculate a Q/B of 17.18
y−1. Diet composition was estimated using Cherel and Duhamel (2003) (Table A.22).
A.12 Fish
We created individual groups for ﬁsh targeted by ﬁsheries and organised remaining species into groups based
on their depth range and size. Mesopelagic ﬁsh were deﬁned as occurring in depths between 0-1000 m and
demersal as species occurring greater than 1000 m depth. Small ﬁsh were classiﬁed as ﬁsh under 50 cm in
length and large ﬁsh were classiﬁed as species greater than 50 cm following Pruvost et al. (2005). Lengths
were mostly taken from Pruvost et al. (2005) unless the species was not listed, then it was taken from ﬁshbase
(https://www.fishbase.se/search.php). Recurring population surveys on the Kerguelen EEZ were
used to estimate biomass estimates for groups in the model (POKER2006, POKER2010, POKER2013). For
clarity , we have only listed species that have been reported in these surveys. For a full list of species occurring
on the Kerguelen Plateau, we refer the reader to Pruvost et al. (2005).

A.12.1 Patagonian toothﬁsh
Patagonian toothﬁsh were separated into juvenile and adult toothﬁsh using the multistanza grouping in Eco-
path. Two studies were used to calculate a minimum and maximum biomass for Patagonian toothﬁsh (under
75 cm) on the Kerguelen Plateau. Juvenile toothﬁsh are described as ﬁsh under 75 cm (Collins et al., 2007),
therefore we used these estimates for juvenile toothﬁsh. Minimum biomass was reported in tonnes, which
was divided over the survey area to give biomass in t km−2. P /B is needed for both life stages of the group,
however, only one biomass and Q/B value were needed when using the multistanza grouping. We provided
these for juvenile toothﬁsh and let Ecopath estimate adult toothﬁsh biomass and Q/B (Table A.23). In addi-
tion to consumption and production parameters, the multi-stanza group for Patagonian toothﬁsh requires a
growth coefﬁcient (K). We used K= 0.12, taken from Ashford et al. (2005). Diet composition was adopted
from Pruvost et al. (2005) (Table A.27).

A.12.2 Mackerel iceﬁsh
We used POKER surveys and random stratiﬁed trawl surveys on the Kerguelen Islands and Heard and McDon-
ald Island, respectively , to estimate biomass for mackerel iceﬁsh. Mackerel iceﬁsh biomass on the Kerguelen
Islands was reported in tonnes and divided by the area of the survey to give 0.092 t km −2. This estimate
seemed too low, especially considering their frequent occurrence in the diets of predators and the operation
of the ﬁshery . Long-term average biomass (in kg km−2) on Heard and McDonald was divided by 1000 to give
0.529 t km−2. This estimate was used as an initial biomass for mackerel iceﬁsh. Production, consumption,
and diet parameters were taken from other studies or models (Table A.24). Diet composition was taken from
119
Appendix A
Hill et al. (2012) (Table A.27).
A.12.3 Myctophids
This group comprises all lanternﬁsh species occurring on the Kerguelen Plateau. There have been no surveys
to estimate lantenrﬁsh biomass, therefore we used biomass estimates from other models and studies. Lam
and Pauly (2005) estimated lanternﬁsh biomass at 4.5 t km−2 in the sub-Antarctic. We have chosen to use this
as an initial estimate for our model. We took P/B and Q/B from Pruvost et al. (2005) for small pelagic ﬁshes
as initial parameters for myctophids. Diet was taken from Gurney et al. (2014). These values are presented
in Table A.24.
A.12.4 Small mesopelagic ﬁsh
We used an initial estimate of 3.2 t km−2 from Hill et al. (2012). We took P /B and Q/B from Pruvost et al.
(2005) and diet composition from Gurney et al. (2014) (Tables A.27 and A.24).

A.12.5 Large mesopelagic ﬁsh
In lieu of a better estimate we used the same estimated biomass for other demersal ﬁsh to parameterise this
group. P/B and Q/B was taken from Pruvost et al. (2005) or calculated using empirical formulas (Froese and
Binohlan, 2000; Palomares and Pauly, 1998). The resulting P/B and Q/B estimates were similar to those for
the large pelagic ﬁsh group in (Pruvost et al., 2005). Diet composition was taken from Gurney et al. (2014)
(Table A.27).

A.13 Krill
Macrozooplankton (> 20 mm in length) were split into two groups based on a study by Hunt et al. (2011). We
estimated macrozooplankton biomass by digitising Fig. 2 in Hunt et al. (2011). These values are presented
in Table A.28. We used this study to split biomass into krill and other macrozooplankton. Euphausiid species
in the study were Euphausia vallentini, Euphausia triacantha and Thysanoessa spp. (mostly T . vicina) and
comprised 63% of biomass. Therefore, average biomass in Table A.28 was multiplied 0.63 to give 20.71 t
km−2 for krill (Table A.28.
P/B was taken from growth rates of euphausiid species. Siegel (1987) reported that growth rates ofE. frigida,
E. crystallorophias, and Thysanoessa macrura are very similar. We took the growth rate used by Pinkerton
et al. (2010) for E. crystallorophias of 1.8 y−1. This was higher than values used for other models by Gurney
et al. (2014), Subramaniam et al. (2019) (0.95 y−1) and Ballerini et al. (2014) (0.45), however, Cartes and
Maynou (1998) suggest higher P/B values for Southern Ocean euphausiids, typically between 1.24–4.75 y−1.
Therefore, this estimate is within the range of estimates assumed for euphausiid species.
Q/B was calculated using a study on euphausiid grazing on Marion Island during austral autumn. Ingestion
rates were taken from Table 7 in Bernard and Froneman (2006). Ingestion rates were averaged over the 5
species in the study , converted to biomass and divided over the average of the biomass of the species giving
a value of 33.16 y−1. This is higher than values estimated in other models but similar to the value estimated
by Bradford-Grieve et al. (2003) therefore, we have chosen to keep this value as a starting estimate and as a
maximum Q/B for the area.
We assumed that consumption of phytoplankton and zooplankton by euphausiids was almost equal. We based

A.14 Other macrozooplankton
Hunt et al. (2011) noted other macrozooplankton consisted of amphipods, chaetognaths, pteropods and salps.
We estimated the biomass of this group by multiplying the remaining percentage composition (37%) by the
average biomass in Table A.28 to give 12.16 t km−2 (Table A.29).
We used the growth rate of amphipod species Themisto gaudichaudii to estimate P /B for other macrozoo-
plankton. T . gaudichaudiiis a common species on the plateau (Hunt et al., 2011; Labat et al., 2005; Bocher
et al., 2001). We took the growth rate 4.87 y−1 from Hill et al. (2012) for T . gaudichaudii.
Q/B was calculated using a grazing study on the Prince Edwards archipelago (Froneman et al., 2002). Average
biomass was taken from Table 3 in Froneman et al. (2002) and averaged over all stations to give 0.998 gDW
m−2 (rounded) (DW = dry weight). Total consumption in mgDW m−2 d−1 was taken from the same table and
divided by 1000 to give 0.034 gDW m−2 d−1 (rounded). Consumption was divided by biomass and multiplied
by 365 to give Q/B of 12.54 y−1:
Q/B = 0.034
0.999× 365
This value was slightly lower than the range of values computed for other sub-Antarctic models, therefore we
considered this value as a minimum estimate for this group. Note that resulting Q /B was calculated using
un-rounded average biomass and consumption from Froneman et al. (2002). Diet composition was taken
from Gurney et al. (2014) (all parameters for other macrozooplankton are presented in Table A.29).

Appendix A
A.15 Mesozooplankton
A.15.1 Biomass
Mesozooplankton (0.2–20 mm) studies report that copepods are the dominant group on the plateau with
remaining mesozooplankton largely representing larval stages of macrozooplankton (Carlotti et al., 2015;
Mayzaud et al., 2002b; Carlotti et al., 2008; Labat et al., 2002). As a consequence we chose to put all
mesozooplankton species into one group. We used six studies sufﬁciently covering the waters surrounding
the plateau to calculate a range of biomasses for the Kerguelen Plateau:
1. An average of 2.32 gC m−2 was given in KEOPS2 during October-November 2011 (Carlotti et al., 2015),
biomass was converted to dry weight using the carbon conversion speciﬁed in the study (carbon biomass
was 50% of biomass) and divided by 0.15 to get a wet weight of 30.93 t km−2 (conversion to wet
weight, pers comm. Kerrie Swadling). This wet weight conversion was similar to the conversions cited
in Atkinson et al. (2012) for mesozooplankton.
2. A study between the Kerguelen Islands and Heard and McDonald Island during January - February 2005
estimated mesozooplankton biomass. An average of transects A, B, and C in Carlotti et al. (2008) was
calculated to give a carbon biomass of 8.51 gC m−2. The same calculation as above was done to give a
wet weight of 113.51 t km−2.
3. A study on the West side of the Kerguelen Plateau during October-November 1995 (Mayzaud et al.,
2002a) estimated a dry weight of 3.65 g m−2, which converts to a wet weight of 24.33 t km−2.
4. A study on the West side of the Kerguelen Plateau during September-October 1995 (Mayzaud et al.,
2002b) estimated a dry weight of 8.3 g m−2, giving a wet weight of 55.33 t km−2.
5. Cruise (ANTARES4) on the north-west side of the Kerguelen Islands during January - February 1999
(Labat et al., 2002) estimated a dry weight of 0.0336 g m−2, giving a wet weight of 44.8 t km−2.
6. Survey of zooplankton on Morhiban Bay in Kerguelen Islands in 1972 gave a summer average of 362.45
mg m-3 and winter average of 46.06 g m-3. We averaged summer and winter values, multiplied by
maximum depth surveyed (300 m) and converted to grams to get an estimated annual biomass of
61.28 t km−2. Philippon (1972) unpublished thesis in Raymont (1983).
This gives a range of 24.33 - 113.51 t km−2. We compared these estimates to initial estimates used in other
studies and decided to use the most recent value from Carlotti et al. (2015) as an initial estimate (highlighted
in bold above).
A.15.2 Production (P /B)
Production was estimated using carbon production rates for copepods. Yearly production was calculated
using total production (mgC m−2 d−1) in the permanently open ocean zone (POOZ) (Table 7 in Mayzaud
et al. 2002b). This was converted to t km−2 y−1 and divided over the biomass calculated above for the same
study (24.33 t km−2). Our initial value is similar to initial values used in Hill et al. (2012); Gurney et al.
(2014) and Ballerini et al. (2014).
Calculation: 50.66× 2× 365
24.33 = 10.13y−1
124
Appendix A
A.15.3 Consumption (Q /B)
Consumption was calculated using a copepod grazing study . We calculated the average carbon ingested per
day (µgC m−3 from Table IV in Razouls et al. 1998) and converted to t km−2 y−1 using depth surveyed in the
study .
Ca× G× DW× 365
WW
where Ca is average carbon ingested (µgC m−3), G is conversion to grams, DW is conversion to dry weight,
and WW is conversion to wet weight.
A.16 Microzooplankton
A range of microzooplankton biomasses were calculated using two studies. Biomass was calculated using two
studies using the equation below. We took average biomass (in mgC m−3) from polar front and permanently
open ocean zone in Table 2 and 3 in Becquevort et al. (2000) and total biomass from Table 7 in Christaki
et al. (2015). Initial input for microzooplankton is provided in Table A.30.
Biomass (t km−2):
B = Bc× D× 10
1000
where Bc is biomass in mgC m−3, D is survey depth, C is carbon conversion of 1g:0.1gC (Gasol et al., 1997).
Microzooplankton has been reported to grow on average 0.1 d −1 in the Ross Sea (Caron et al., 2000). We
took this growth rate and multiplied by 365 to give a P /B of 36.5 y−1. This was within the range of growth
rates used for other models (Ballerini et al., 2014; Bradford-Grieve et al., 2003; Hill et al., 2012; Pinkerton
et al., 2010).
Q/B was determined using a study on microzooplankton grazing on the plateau. Average grazing rate was
calculated from Table 3 in Brussaard et al. (2008) (excluding zeros) giving 0.427 d−1. This was multiplied by
365 to give 155.73 y−1. Diet composition was taken from Pinkerton et al. (2010) (Table A.30).

Appendix A
A.17 Bacteria
A.17.1 Biomass
Bacteria were surveyed on the Kerguelen Islands during ANTARES 3 and ANTARES 4 cruises. Bacteria were
sampled in the surface layer providing an estimate of biomass (mgC m−3) for the Polar Front (PFr) and the
Permanently Open Ocean Zone (POOZ). We took an average of these values from Tables 8 and 9 in Becquevort
et al. (2000) and converted them to wet weight in t km −2 to provide an annual average for the Kerguelen
Plateau (Table A.31).
Wet weight in t km−2 was calculated by:
B = Ba× D
1000 × C
where Ba is average biomass in mgC m−3, D is depth surveyed (both provided in Table A.31), and C is the
carbon conversion of 1g of bacteria = 0.1gC.
Table A.31. Biomass parameters for bacteria
Parameters Reference
Fraction of year present 1
Biomass (Ba) (14.8+11.4)/2 = 13.1 (10.4+5.3)/2 = 7.85 Becquevort et al. (2000)
Survey year February 1994 (late summer) October 1995 (early spring)
Survey site South-west Kerguelen South-west Kerguelen
Depth surveyed (D) 100 138 (average of all depths)
Biomass (t km−2) 13.1 10.833
A.17.2 Other parameters
Growth rates were used as production parameters for bacteria and are commonly published in the literature.
We compiled a range of growth rates from the literature and referenced them with food web models that
included bacteria to choose an appropriate starting value. Using growth rates, consumption was calculated
using production/consumption ratios (P/Q) reported in the literature.
We calculated three options for P/B from three different studies of bacteria:
1. Becquevort et al. (2000) reported bacteria biomass as well as microzooplankton biomass in their study .
Average bacterial production was 35 mgC m−2 d−1, bacterial biomass for the whole area was 7.4 mgC
m−3, which was multiplied by depth to give 1021.2 mgC m−2. P/B = (35/1021.2)× 365 = 12.5 d−1.
2. The ﬁrst studied bacterial growth at Davis station in Antarctica. We averaged total bacteria (d−1) from
Table 5 in Pearce et al. (2008) and multiplied by 365 to get a P/B of 22.85 y−1.
3. Bacterial growth studied on the Kerguelen Islands from January to February 2005. We averaged bacte-
rial growth d−1 from Table 1 in Landa et al. (2016) and multiplied by 365 to give 51.47 y−1
P/Q ratios were taken from two studies, the ﬁrst study that was also used by Hill et al. (2012) and Pinkerton
et al. (2010) and from a study on the bacterial growth efﬁciencies on the Kerguelen Plateau (Obernosterer
et al., 2008). P /Q for Obernosterer et al. (2008) was calculated by averaging bacterial growth efﬁciencies
126
Appendix A
(%) over the whole study ((14+20+10)/3). Q/B was calculated by:
Q/B = P/B
P/Q
We compared P/B values in Table A.31 to those calculated for other models that included bacteria. Hill et al.
(2012) used a value of 20 y−1 for their model, Pinkerton et al. (2010) used a value of 35 y−1, and Bradford-
Grieve et al. (2003) used a value of 87 y−1. Due to the large range of values used for other models, we took
the average of these values to get a P /B of 47.33 yy−1, this was closest to the value calculated using Landa
et al. (2016), therefore we used this as a starting estimate. We did the same for Q/B values, Hill et al. (2012)
used a value of 66.67 y−1, Pinkerton et al. (2010) used a value of 118 y−1, and Bradford-Grieve et al. (2003)
used a value of 383.33 y−1, giving an average of 189.33 y−1. This was again closest to our value calculated
using Landa et al. (2016) and a P /Q of 0.3, therefore we used this as an initial value. We assumed bacteria
consumed only detritus in the model.

A.18 Phytoplankton
A.18.1 Biomass
We calculated a range of biomasses using in situ studies and satellite data. Biomass values estimated from in
situ studies are presented in Table A.34. We took satellite chlorophyll-a (Chl a) from Fig. 2 in Dragon et al.
(2011) (see Table A.33 as winter values were provided).
A variety of carbon to chla ratios (C:Chl a) have been used to convert satellite Chla to wet biomass. We used
these ratios and the equation below to calculate a range of initial phytoplankton biomasses using average chl
a from Dragon et al. (2011) (see Table A.35). Pinkerton et al. (2010) reported a C:Chl a ratio of 80 to 100
for sub-Antarctic waters. Fiala et al. (1998a) found an average ratio of 95 on the west side of the Kerguelen
Islands. We used this ratio to estimate an initial phytoplankton biomass of 38.76 t km−2.
Biomass was estimated by:
B = BC× Zeu× C
1000
where BC is carbon biomass in mgC m−3, Zeu is the depth of the euphotic zone (we used a depth of 120 m
following Gurney et al. 2014) and C is carbon to wet weight conversion (x10, Gurney et al. 2014).
A.18.2 Production
We estimated P/B using a multiple studies of chla production per day and converting this to yearly rate (Table
A.36). Becquevort et al. (2000) production per day was converted to wet weight and divided by biomass to
give P/B (y−1). Rates provided as d−1 were multiplied by 365 to give an annual rate. We took an average for

A.19 Macroalgae
The most dominant species of macroalgae on the Kerguelen Islands isMacrocystis pyrifera. Macrocystis pyrifera
are found near the coast of the Kerguelen Islands, forming giant kelp forests that protect marine invertebrates
and ﬁsh from predators (Koubbi et al., 2016). Macroalgal biomass was updated from Subramaniam et al.
(2019) using remote sensing data in Morbihan Bay , where average biomass was 22.5 kg m −2 (wet weight,
Delille et al. 2009). We took total biomass estimated as 1,100,000 tonnes (1100 kilotonnes) and divided
biomass over the area of the model domain to give 0.7 t km−2. Studies suggest production of Macrocystis
species is high, therefore used P /B from Gurney et al. (2014) for M. levis calculated from a study of growth
on the Prince Edwards Islands Attwood et al. (1991).
P/B for the model was calculated as follows: mean biomass in Attwood et al. (1991) was converted to gC
m−2 and divided by net production (gC m−2) for April and August to give P/B y−1. Average P/B for April and
August was 5.22 y−1.
A.20 Detritus and Carcasses
The biomass of detritus was calculated using an empirical formula described in Christensen and Pauly (1993).
The equation was re-arranged from:
log 10D =−2.41 + 0.95log 10PP + 0.863log 10Zeu
To:
D = 10−2.4+0.9log 10PP +0.863log 10Zeu
where D is detritus (gC m−2), PP is phytoplankton production (gC m−2 y−1) and E is depth of the euphotic
zone in metres. D was multiplied by 9 following Gurney et al. (2014) to give detrital biomass in t km−2.
Uitz et al. (2009) measured primary production for different size classes of phytoplankton on the Kerguelen
Plateau from January to February 2005. We took total primary production from Table 3 in Uitz et al. (2009)
and averaged over all stations to give a value of 0.61 gC m−2 d−1. This was multiplied by 365 to give yearly
production (PP) of 222.65 gC m −2 y−1. Depth of the euphotic zone varied between each site therefore,
we calculated average euphotic depth using the maximum of each range found for each site to give Zeu =
(40+34+90)/3. This gave a detrital biomass of 197.61 gC m−2. This compares to the value used by Pruvost
et al. (2005). We did not estimate biomass for carcasses in the model.

A.22 Pre-balance diagnostics
We used the pre-balance diagnostics described in Link (2010) and Heymans et al. (2016). We used this
diagnostic to test the underlying assumptions used to estimate biomass for functional groups (Fig. A.1.).
Biomass should increase as trophic level decreases and groups that depart far above or below the line of best
ﬁt needs to be checked for data quality . Balanced biomasses in our model show an increase in the order of
21%, which is above the guideline of 10% but follows general trophic decomposition (Link, 2010).

B.3 Comparison of model ﬁts to ﬁshery-observer data
Plots of individuals counted per day per year from Gasco et al. (2019) were digitised and plotted for com-
parison with modelled biomass trends. Table B.5 references comparisons of trends for each of the models
described in this study . As can be seen in Table B.5, the preferred model reproduced ﬁshery-observer trends
better than the model with the original vulnerabilities. Note that the observer trend for killer whales (Figs
B.6 and B.11) is reﬂective of the trend presented in Gasco et al. (2019) however, the values are not. The data
presented in Gasco et al. (2019) did not have clear counts that could be digitised. Therefore, the trend was
reproduced by assuming that the lowest count was zero and the highest count was 1.
Table B.5. Comparison of model ﬁts to ﬁshery-observer data
Model 3 with original vulnerabilities Preferred model with replacement vulnerabilities

Appendix C
C.3 Results
Baleen whales biomass was concentrated from the north-west to the east outside of the Kerguelen Plateau (Fig
C.5). This is consistent with tracking study showing one humpback whale visited east side of the Kerguelen
Plateau at the boundary of the French EEZ (Bestley et al., 2019).
Figure C.5. Biomass distribution from Ecospace models for baleen whales (kg km−2). a) habitat importance
map used to parameterise baleen whale distribution b) baleen whale distribution in c) non-ﬁshed scenario
and c) ﬁshed scenario.
Spatial correlations showed that modelled output closely matched observations (Table C.1).
